### Accident reporting

#### P107-034

**Q:** Part 107 requires reporting to the FAA within 10 calendar days for an operation that involves:

- **A.** Any scratch to the drone
- **B.** Serious injury or any loss of consciousness ✅
- **C.** Any flight over 200 ft AGL

**✅ Answer:** B — Serious injury or any loss of consciousness

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from 14 CFR 107.9(a).

_Citation: 14 CFR 107.9(a) | Source: part107-question-bank | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part107-bank, accident_reporting_

---

#### P107-035

**Q:** Part 107 requires reporting within 10 calendar days if property damage (other than the sUAS) is:

- **A.** Exactly $500
- **B.** More than $500 to repair, OR more than $500 fair market value in total loss ✅
- **C.** Any amount if the drone is under 250 grams

**✅ Answer:** B — More than $500 to repair, OR more than $500 fair market value in total loss

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from 14 CFR 107.9(b).

_Citation: 14 CFR 107.9(b) | Source: part107-question-bank | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part107-bank, accident_reporting_

### Acronyms & Abbreviations

#### ACR-001

**Q:** What does the abbreviation "14 CFR" stand for?

- **A.** Title 14 of the Code of Federal Regulations. ✅
- **B.** 14th Circuit Federal Register.
- **C.** Title 14 Civil Flight Rules.

**✅ Answer:** A — Title 14 of the Code of Federal Regulations.

**Explanation:** 14 CFR stands for Title 14 of the Code of Federal Regulations — the title containing all federal aviation regulations including Part 107.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** There is no '14th Circuit Federal Register.' CFR stands for Code of Federal Regulations.
- **C:** CFR does not stand for Civil Flight Rules. It is the Code of Federal Regulations.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, cfr, regulations_

---

#### ACR-002

**Q:** In FAA publications, what does "AC" stand for?

- **A.** Advisory Circular. ✅
- **B.** Air Carrier.
- **C.** Aircraft Certificate.

**✅ Answer:** A — Advisory Circular.

**Explanation:** AC stands for Advisory Circular — FAA informational documents that provide guidance and best practices on aviation topics.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Air Carrier is not abbreviated AC in FAA terminology. AC refers to Advisory Circular.
- **C:** Aircraft Certificate is not the meaning of AC. Advisory Circulars are FAA guidance documents.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: FAA Advisory Circular system | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, ac, advisory-circular_

#### ACR-003

**Q:** What does "ACS" stand for in the context of FAA pilot testing?

- **A.** Airman Certification Standards. ✅
- **B.** Aircraft Control System.
- **C.** Air Communication Service.

**✅ Answer:** A — Airman Certification Standards.

**Explanation:** ACS stands for Airman Certification Standards — the document that defines the knowledge, risk management, and skills required for FAA pilot certificates and ratings.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Aircraft Control System is not what ACS stands for in the testing context.
- **C:** ACS does not refer to a communication service. It defines certification testing standards.

#### ACR-007

**Q:** What does "AFM" stand for?

- **A.** Airplane Flight Manual. ✅
- **B.** Aviation Frequency Management.
- **C.** Air Force Manual.

**✅ Answer:** A — Airplane Flight Manual.

**Explanation:** AFM stands for Airplane Flight Manual — the FAA-approved document containing operating limitations, procedures, and performance data for a specific aircraft.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** AFM does not refer to frequency management. It is the official flight manual for an aircraft.
- **C:** In aviation context, AFM means Airplane Flight Manual, not Air Force Manual.

#### ACR-008

**Q:** What does "AFS" stand for in FAA organizational structure?

- **A.** Flight Standards Service. ✅
- **B.** Airport Facility Survey.
- **C.** Airfield Fire Service.

**✅ Answer:** A — Flight Standards Service.

**Explanation:** AFS stands for Flight Standards Service — the FAA office responsible for certification and oversight of airmen, air operators, and air agencies.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** AFS is not an airport survey. It refers to the FAA's Flight Standards Service.
- **C:** AFS is not a fire service. It is the Flight Standards Service within the FAA.

- **A.** Aviation Training Device. ✅
- **B.** Air Transport Document.
- **C.** Airport Terminal District.

**✅ Answer:** A — Aviation Training Device.

**Explanation:** ATD stands for Aviation Training Device — a training tool that replicates aircraft instruments and controls for ground-based practice.

**Q:** What does "CFR" stand for?

- **A.** Code of Federal Regulations. ✅
- **B.** Certified Flight Requirements.
- **C.** Central Flight Record.

**✅ Answer:** A — Code of Federal Regulations.

**Explanation:** CFR stands for Code of Federal Regulations — the codification of the general and permanent rules published in the Federal Register by U.S. executive departments and agencies.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** CFR does not stand for Certified Flight Requirements. It is the Code of Federal Regulations.
- **C:** CFR is not a flight record. It is the complete collection of federal regulations.

- **A.** Certification Training Program. ✅
- **B.** Commercial Transport Pilot.
- **C.** Controlled Traffic Pattern.

**✅ Answer:** A — Certification Training Program.

**Explanation:** CTP stands for Certification Training Program — an FAA-required training program for certain pilot certificates.

- **B:** CTP is not a pilot certificate. It is a training program requirement.

#### ACR-022

- **A.** Designated Pilot Examiner. ✅
- **B.** Drone Performance Evaluation.
- **C.** Departure Procedure Establishment.

**✅ Answer:** A — Designated Pilot Examiner.

**Explanation:** DPE stands for Designated Pilot Examiner — an individual authorized by the FAA to conduct practical tests for pilot certificates and ratings.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** DPE is not about drone evaluation. A DPE conducts pilot checkrides on behalf of the FAA.
- **C:** DPE has nothing to do with departure procedures. It is a Designated Pilot Examiner.

#### ACR-034

**Q:** What does "ICAO" stand for?

- **A.** International Civil Aviation Organization. ✅
- **B.** International Commercial Airline Operations.

#### ACR-041

**Q:** What does "MEL" stand for?

- **A.** Minimum Equipment List. ✅
- **B.** Maximum Elevation Level.
- **C.** Military Equipment Listing.

**✅ Answer:** A — Minimum Equipment List.

**Explanation:** MEL stands for Minimum Equipment List — a document that allows an aircraft to be operated with certain items of equipment inoperative under specific conditions.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MEL is not an elevation reference. It lists equipment that may be inoperative for dispatch.
- **C:** MEL has nothing to do with military equipment. It is a Minimum Equipment List.

**Q:** What does "MFD" stand for?

- **A.** Multi-functional Displays. ✅
- **B.** Minimum Flight Distance.
- **C.** Military Flight Directive.

**✅ Answer:** A — Multi-functional Displays.

**Explanation:** MFD stands for Multi-functional Displays — cockpit screens that can show multiple types of information including navigation, weather, and traffic.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MFD is not a distance measurement. It refers to electronic display screens.
- **C:** MFD has nothing to do with military directives. It is a cockpit display system.

**Q:** What does "NACG" stand for?

- **A.** National Aeronautical Charting Group. ✅
- **B.** National Aviation Control Grid.
- **C.** Non-standard Approach Control Group.

**✅ Answer:** A — National Aeronautical Charting Group.

**Explanation:** NACG stands for National Aeronautical Charting Group — the organization responsible for producing aeronautical charts including sectional and terminal area charts.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** NACG is not a control grid. It produces the charts pilots use for navigation.
- **C:** NACG has nothing to do with approach control. It is the charting organization.

#### ACR-047

**Q:** What does "NASA" stand for?

- **A.** National Aeronautics and Space Administration. ✅
- **B.** National Airspace Safety Authority.
- **C.** North American Surveillance Agency.

**✅ Answer:** A — National Aeronautics and Space Administration.

**Explanation:** NASA stands for National Aeronautics and Space Administration. Pilots may file confidential safety reports through NASA's ASRS (Aviation Safety Reporting System).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** NASA is not a safety authority. It is the space and aeronautics research agency.

#### ACR-055

**Q:** What does "POH" stand for?

- **A.** Pilot's Operating Handbook. ✅
- **B.** Primary Operations Hub.
- **C.** Post-Operation Hazard.

**✅ Answer:** A — Pilot's Operating Handbook.

**Explanation:** POH stands for Pilot's Operating Handbook — the document containing operating procedures and performance data for a specific aircraft model.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** POH is not a facility. It is the operating manual for the aircraft.
- **C:** POH has nothing to do with hazards. It is the Pilot's Operating Handbook.

#### ACR-068

**Q:** What does "VO" stand for under Part 107?

- **A.** Visual Observer. ✅
- **B.** Verification Officer.
- **C.** Variable Output.

**✅ Answer:** A — Visual Observer.

**Explanation:** VO stands for Visual Observer — an optional crewmember who helps maintain situational awareness by watching the aircraft and scanning for hazards.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** VO is not a verification officer. It is a crew role in sUAS operations.
- **C:** VO does not refer to equipment output. It is the Visual Observer role.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.33 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, vo, crew-roles_

#### ACR-071

**Q:** What does the abbreviation "RPIC" stand for in Part 107 operations?

- **A.** Remote Pilot in Command. ✅
- **B.** Registered Pilot Identification Code.
- **C.** Remote Pilot Instruction Certificate.

**✅ Answer:** A — Remote Pilot in Command.

**Explanation:** RPIC stands for Remote Pilot in Command — the person who has final authority and responsibility for the operation and safety of a sUAS flight under Part 107.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** There is no FAA term 'Registered Pilot Identification Code.' RPIC refers to Remote Pilot in Command.
- **C:** RPIC does not stand for Instruction Certificate. It is the designated person in command of the flight.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.12 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, rpic, remote-pilot_

#### ACR-072

**Q:** In FAA terminology, what does "sUAS" stand for?

- **A.** Small Unmanned Aircraft System. ✅
- **B.** Standard Unmanned Aerial Surveillance.
- **C.** Small Utility Aircraft Service.

**✅ Answer:** A — Small Unmanned Aircraft System.

**Explanation:** sUAS stands for Small Unmanned Aircraft System. Under Part 107, this generally means a drone weighing less than 55 pounds at takeoff, including everything on board or attached.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** sUAS does not refer to surveillance. It describes the category of small unmanned aircraft systems under Part 107.
- **C:** sUAS is not related to utility services. It is the regulatory term for small drones.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.3 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, suas, definitions_

---

#### ACR-075

**Q:** What does "VLOS" stand for?

- **A.** Visual Line of Sight. ✅
- **B.** Variable Level Operating Standard.
- **C.** Vertical Launch and Operations System.

**✅ Answer:** A — Visual Line of Sight.

**Explanation:** VLOS stands for Visual Line of Sight — the requirement that the RPIC, the person manipulating the controls, or a VO must see the unmanned aircraft throughout the flight with unaided vision (except corrective lenses).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** VLOS is not an operating standard level. It is a visual tracking requirement.
- **C:** VLOS has nothing to do with launch systems. It requires visual contact with the drone.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.31 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, vlos, visual-line-of-sight_

---

#### ACR-076

**Q:** What does "BVLOS" stand for?

- **A.** Beyond Visual Line of Sight. ✅
- **B.** Basic Visual Location Operating Standard.
- **C.** Broad Visual Landing Operations System.

**✅ Answer:** A — Beyond Visual Line of Sight.

**Explanation:** BVLOS stands for Beyond Visual Line of Sight — operating a drone when the crew cannot directly see it. This is not routine under Part 107 and generally requires a waiver.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** BVLOS is not a location standard. It describes operating beyond the pilot's visual range.
- **C:** BVLOS has nothing to do with landing. It means the drone is beyond unaided visual range.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.31 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, bvlos, advanced-operations_

### Alcohol and drugs

#### P107-063

**Q:** Under Part 107, a person acting as RPIC, VO, or manipulating the controls must comply with alcohol and drug provisions of:

- **A.** 14 CFR 91.17 and 91.19 ✅
- **B.** 49 CFR 171.8 only
- **C.** 14 CFR 61.57 only

**✅ Answer:** A — 14 CFR 91.17 and 91.19

### Authorizations vs waivers

#### P107-075

**Q:** Which statement best describes the difference between 'authorization' and 'waiver' in Part 107?

- **A.** Authorization is for controlled airspace access; a waiver is permission to deviate from certain Part 107 rules ✅
- **B.** They are the same thing
- **C.** A waiver is for airspace; authorization is for night operations only

**✅ Answer:** A — Authorization is for controlled airspace access; a waiver is permission to deviate from certain Part 107 rules

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from 14 CFR 107.41 and 107.200.

_Citation: 14 CFR 107.41 and 107.200 | Source: part107-question-bank | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part107-bank, authorizations_vs_waivers_

#### P107-072

**Q:** Does a Part 107 Remote Pilot Certificate have an expiration date printed on it?

- **A.** Yes, it expires every 24 months
- **B.** No, the certificate is permanent unless revoked, but you must maintain aeronautical knowledge recency ✅

**✅ Answer:** B — No, the certificate is permanent unless revoked, but you must maintain aeronautical knowledge recency

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from FAA Part 107 certificate expiration FAQ / 14 CFR 107.65.

_Citation: FAA Part 107 certificate expiration FAQ / 14 CFR 107.65 | Source: part107-question-bank | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part107-bank, certification_

- **C.** Yes, but only if requested

**✅ Answer:** B — No, the certificate does not expire; you keep proof of completion as evidence of currency

### Night Operations

#### PRAC-001

**Q:** You are flying a small unmanned aircraft at night and observe an airplane flying at low altitude in your area. How can you determine if the aircraft is getting closer or moving farther away?

- **A.** The aircraft will appear to be descending as it moves closer.
- **B.** The lights on the aircraft will increase or decrease in intensity. ✅
- **C.** The aircraft's lights will appear to change colors.

**✅ Answer:** B — The lights on the aircraft will increase or decrease in intensity.

**Explanation:** At night, an aircraft that is getting closer will generally appear brighter because its lights increase in intensity. Dimming lights usually indicate the aircraft is moving farther away.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Relative descent alone is not a reliable nighttime cue for whether another aircraft is closing on your position.
- **C:** Navigation light colors indicate aircraft orientation, not whether it is getting closer or farther away.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K14b | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17; AIM 8-1-6 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: remote pilot, night operations, aircraft lights, collision avoidance_

### Operating Limitations

#### PRAC-009

**Q:** A recreational drone pilot is flying in Class G airspace near a 600-foot radio tower. Under which condition, if any, may the pilot legally fly above 400 feet AGL?

- **A.** Never; a recreational flyer is not allowed to exceed 400 feet AGL. ✅
- **B.** If the pilot remains within 400 feet laterally of the tower.
- **C.** If the tower owner grants permission and visual line of sight is maintained.

**✅ Answer:** A — Never; a recreational flyer is not allowed to exceed 400 feet AGL.

**Explanation:** Under the recreational exception, the pilot must operate within the fixed altitude limit and does not receive the Part 107 structure-based allowance above 400 feet AGL. The scenario does not create an exception.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The structure-based altitude exception is not part of the recreational rule set described in this question.
- **C:** Property-owner permission does not create a recreational altitude exception.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K3 | Citation: 49 U.S.C. §44809 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: drone, class g, recreational rules, altitude limits_

---

#### PRAC-011

**Q:** A remote pilot obtained a remote pilot certificate after previously registering a small unmanned aircraft for recreational use. The pilot now plans to conduct a paid aerial photography flight with the same aircraft. What action is required before conducting the flight?

- **A.** No action is required because the aircraft may be used commercially under the same recreational registration.
- **B.** The aircraft must be re-registered under Part 107 before it can be used for commercial operations. ✅
- **C.** Only the pilot certificate must be updated; the aircraft registration does not change.

**✅ Answer:** B — The aircraft must be re-registered under Part 107 before it can be used for commercial operations.

**Explanation:** Commercial use requires the aircraft to be registered for Part 107 operations. A recreational registration does not cover the aircraft once it is used for compensation or hire.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Recreational registration does not automatically authorize commercial use of the aircraft.
- **C:** The aircraft registration itself must reflect the new operational context.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR Part 48; FAA DroneZone Registration Guidance | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: remote pilot, registration, commercial use, part 107_

---

#### PRAC-012

**Q:** A non-U.S. citizen is visiting the United States and plans to conduct a commercial drone operation using a drone that is registered with the FAA. Which statement is correct?

- **A.** The operation is permitted if the drone is FAA registered and the operator holds an FAA remote pilot certificate. ✅
- **B.** The operation is prohibited because only U.S. citizens may operate FAA-registered drones.
- **C.** The operation is permitted only if the drone was manufactured in the United States.

**✅ Answer:** A — The operation is permitted if the drone is FAA registered and the operator holds an FAA remote pilot certificate.

**Explanation:** Part 107 operations are not limited to U.S. citizens. If the operator holds the required FAA remote pilot certification and the aircraft is properly registered, the commercial operation may be conducted lawfully.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Citizenship alone does not bar a properly certificated remote pilot from conducting a lawful operation.
- **C:** The country of manufacture does not determine whether a Part 107 operation is permitted.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §§107.12, 48.15 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: remote pilot, registration, commercial operation, part 107_

### Responsibilities

#### P107-005

**Q:** Under Part 107, the Remote Pilot in Command (RPIC) is:

- **A.** The person who paid for the drone
- **B.** Directly responsible for and the final authority for the operation ✅
- **C.** Only responsible if an accident occurs

**✅ Answer:** B — Directly responsible for and the final authority for the operation

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from 14 CFR 107.19(b).

_Citation: 14 CFR 107.19(b) | Source: part107-question-bank | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part107-bank, responsibilities_

---

#### P107-074

**Q:** Under Part 107, the RPIC must ensure that in the event of a loss of control, the sUAS will pose:

- **A.** No hazard to other people, aircraft, or property
- **B.** No undue hazard to other people, aircraft, or property ✅
- **C.** Only a hazard to property, not people

**✅ Answer:** B — No undue hazard to other people, aircraft, or property

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from 14 CFR 107.19(c).

_Citation: 14 CFR 107.19(c) | Source: part107-question-bank | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part107-bank, responsibilities_

---

#### PRAC-003

**Q:** Two certified remote pilots want to transfer control of a small UA during active flight. How may this be accomplished?

- **A.** There can only be one remote pilot who maintains control and authority at a time during the transfer. ✅
- **B.** By sharing the control responsibility at the same time.
- **C.** Only by landing the aircraft before transferring control.

**✅ Answer:** A — There can only be one remote pilot who maintains control and authority at a time during the transfer.

**Explanation:** Only one person may act as remote PIC at a time. Control may be transferred during flight, but a single remote pilot must maintain operational authority and responsibility throughout the handoff.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Part 107 does not allow shared simultaneous command authority over the same aircraft.
- **C:** Landing is not required if the transfer is managed so that one remote pilot clearly holds authority at all times.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K20 | Citation: 14 CFR §§107.12, 107.19 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: remote pilot, transfer of control, responsibility, small ua_

---

#### PRAC-019

**Q:** A remote pilot launches a Part 107 operation using a manufacturer-provided automated flight mode. The aircraft independently adjusts altitude to maintain tracking accuracy and briefly exceeds the authorized altitude without pilot input or warning. Who is responsible for the deviation?

- **A.** The aircraft manufacturer.
- **B.** The remote pilot in command. ✅
- **C.** No one, because the altitude change was automatic.

**✅ Answer:** B — The remote pilot in command.

**Explanation:** The remote PIC remains directly responsible for the operation of the small UAS at all times, including when manufacturer-provided automation or autonomous features are being used.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Manufacturers provide features, but operational compliance remains the responsibility of the remote PIC.
- **C:** Automation does not remove pilot responsibility under Part 107.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K20 | Citation: 14 CFR §§107.19, 107.49 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part 107, remote pic, automation, responsibility_

- **A.** Only airplanes and helicopters
- **B.** All aircraft, airborne vehicles, and launch and reentry vehicles ✅
- **C.** Only aircraft in controlled airspace

**✅ Answer:** B — All aircraft, airborne vehicles, and launch and reentry vehicles

- **A.** May hover in place until the other aircraft passes
- **B.** Must give way and may not pass over, under, or ahead unless well clear ✅
- **C.** May continue its planned route if under 100 ft AGL

### Safety and Compliance

#### PRAC-004

**Q:** If a remote PIC intentionally falsifies a required report, what action may the FAA take?

- **A.** Issue only a warning letter unless property damage occurred.
- **B.** Suspend or revoke any certificate, waiver, or declaration of compliance. ✅
- **C.** Require the remote PIC to retake the knowledge test within 30 days.

**✅ Answer:** B — Suspend or revoke any certificate, waiver, or declaration of compliance.

**Explanation:** Under Part 107, falsification, reproduction, or alteration of applications, certificates, logbooks, reports, or records can lead the FAA to suspend or revoke a certificate, waiver, or declaration of compliance.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Intentional falsification is not limited to a warning letter and is not conditioned on property damage.
- **C:** The regulation authorizes certificate, waiver, or declaration action, not a simple retest requirement.

_ACS: UA.I.A.K4 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.5 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: remote pic, falsification, reports, compliance_

---

### See and Avoid

#### PRAC-002

**Q:** Why would it be unsafe to operate a small unmanned aircraft in the vicinity of hot air balloons?

- **A.** Because of the unpredictability caused by wind.
- **B.** Because of the Coriolis effect caused by the Earth's rotation.
- **C.** Because hot air balloons may be tethered during launch, landing, or ground handling, and the tethered lines can be extremely difficult to see. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Because hot air balloons may be tethered during launch, landing, or ground handling, and the tethered lines can be extremely difficult to see.

**Explanation:** Hot air balloons can be tethered during launch, landing, or ground handling. Those lines can be extremely difficult to detect from a remote pilot station, creating a severe collision hazard for a small UAS.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Wind affects balloons, but the specific hazard described here is the presence of nearly invisible tether lines.
- **B:** The Coriolis effect does not create the immediate operational hazard that tether lines do in the launch and recovery area.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K14 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: remote pilot, see and avoid, hot air balloon, tether lines_

---

#### PRAC-015

**Q:** What visual scanning method should a remote pilot use to effectively detect other aircraft and avoid collisions?

- **A.** Stare at one point in the distance and wait for movement.
- **B.** Use a series of short, regularly spaced eye movements to scan small sections of the sky. ✅
- **C.** Rely primarily on peripheral vision.

**✅ Answer:** B — Use a series of short, regularly spaced eye movements to scan small sections of the sky.

**Explanation:** Effective visual scanning uses short, deliberate eye movements that divide the sky into small sections. That allows the eyes time to focus and improves the chance of detecting conflicting traffic.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Fixating on one point reduces overall traffic detection and can cause conflicting aircraft to be missed.
- **C:** Peripheral vision alone is not reliable for see-and-avoid duties.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K14a | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17; AIM 8-1-6 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: remote pilot, see and avoid, visual scanning, collision avoidance_

- **A.** Binoculars
- **B.** Corrective lenses (glasses/contacts) ✅

### Verified Rules

#### REG-V-001

**Q:** What is the Part 107 default rule for drone operations under 55 lb?

- **A.** Part 107 is the default regulation for most non-recreational operations. ✅
- **B.** Part 101 applies to all drone operations under 55 lb.
- **C.** No FAA regulation applies to drones under 55 lb.

**✅ Answer:** A — Part 107 is the default regulation for most non-recreational operations.

**Explanation:** For drones under 55 lb, Part 107 is the default regulation for most non-recreational operations.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Part 101 covers recreational operations (the exception), not the default rule.
- **C:** FAA regulations do apply to all drone operations in the National Airspace System.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR Part 107 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: regulations, part107, basic_

---

#### REG-V-002

**Q:** What does sUAS stand for and what does it mean under Part 107?

- **A.** Small Unmanned Aircraft System — generally a drone operation under 55 lb at takeoff. ✅
- **B.** Standard Unmanned Aerial System — any drone over 25 lb.
- **C.** Supervised Unmanned Aircraft System — requires a co-pilot.

**✅ Answer:** A — Small Unmanned Aircraft System — generally a drone operation under 55 lb at takeoff.

**Explanation:** sUAS stands for Small Unmanned Aircraft System. Under Part 107 it generally means a drone operation under 55 lb at takeoff.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The 'S' stands for 'small' and the weight limit is 55 lb, not 25 lb.
- **C:** sUAS does not require a co-pilot — it refers only to the weight category.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.3 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: regulations, definitions, suas_

---

#### REG-V-003

**Q:** What is a Remote PIC (RPIC) under Part 107?

- **A.** Remote Pilot in Command. Must be designated before or during the flight and has final authority and responsibility for the operation. ✅
- **B.** A person who monitors airspace from the ground during the flight.
- **C.** An FAA inspector who oversees drone operations.

**✅ Answer:** A — Remote Pilot in Command. Must be designated before or during the flight and has final authority and responsibility for the operation.

**Explanation:** The RPIC is the Remote Pilot in Command, must be designated before or during the flight, and has final authority and responsibility for the operation.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** That describes a Visual Observer (VO), not the RPIC.
- **C:** The RPIC is the operator, not an FAA inspector.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K2 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.12 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: regulations, rpic, roles_

---

#### REG-V-004

**Q:** What is the role of a Visual Observer (VO) under Part 107?

- **A.** An optional crewmember who helps maintain awareness; must be able to see the aircraft and maintain effective communication with the RPIC. ✅
- **B.** A mandatory crewmember who must be present on every Part 107 flight.
- **C.** The person primarily responsible for controlling the drone.

**✅ Answer:** A — An optional crewmember who helps maintain awareness; must be able to see the aircraft and maintain effective communication with the RPIC.

**Explanation:** The VO is optional, helps maintain situational awareness, must maintain VLOS of the aircraft, and must communicate effectively with the RPIC and person manipulating the controls.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The VO is optional under Part 107, not mandatory.
- **C:** The person manipulating the controls operates the drone; the VO is an observer role.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K2 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.33 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: regulations, vo, roles_

---

#### REG-V-005

**Q:** What does VLOS mean and who must maintain it?

- **A.** Visual Line of Sight. The RPIC, person manipulating the controls, or a VO must see the aircraft throughout the flight with unaided vision (corrective lenses allowed). ✅
- **B.** Video Line of Sight. The RPIC may use FPV goggles as the sole means of maintaining sight.
- **C.** Visual Line of Sight. Only the RPIC must maintain it at all times.

**✅ Answer:** A — Visual Line of Sight. The RPIC, person manipulating the controls, or a VO must see the aircraft throughout the flight with unaided vision (corrective lenses allowed).

**Explanation:** VLOS (Visual Line of Sight) requires the RPIC, person manipulating the controls, or a VO to maintain unaided visual sight of the aircraft. Corrective lenses are allowed; FPV-only is not.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** FPV goggles alone do not satisfy the VLOS requirement.
- **C:** A VO may fulfill the VLOS requirement, so the RPIC alone is not the only option.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K3 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.31 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: regulations, vlos, operations_

---

#### REG-V-006

**Q:** What is BVLOS and is it routine under current Part 107?

- **A.** Beyond Visual Line of Sight. Not routine — generally requires an FAA waiver or specific authorization. ✅
- **B.** Beyond Visual Line of Sight. Allowed any time as long as the RPIC holds a Part 107 certificate.
- **C.** Beyond Visual Line of Sight. Allowed routinely at night.

**✅ Answer:** A — Beyond Visual Line of Sight. Not routine — generally requires an FAA waiver or specific authorization.

**Explanation:** BVLOS is Beyond Visual Line of Sight. It is not a routine Part 107 operation and generally requires an FAA waiver or specific authorization.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** A Part 107 certificate alone does not authorize BVLOS operations.
- **C:** Night authorization does not extend to BVLOS operations.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K3 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.31; 107.200 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: regulations, bvlos, waiver_

---

#### REG-V-011

**Q:** What is the difference between a waiver and a COA under Part 107?

- **A.** A waiver permits deviating from specific Part 107 rules. A COA (Certificate of Waiver or Authorization) is typically used for public aircraft or certain Part 91/public safety operations, not the normal Part 107 pathway. ✅
- **B.** A COA is the same as a waiver — the terms are interchangeable.
- **C.** A waiver is only for military drones; a COA is for commercial operators.

**✅ Answer:** A — A waiver permits deviating from specific Part 107 rules. A COA (Certificate of Waiver or Authorization) is typically used for public aircraft or certain Part 91/public safety operations, not the normal Part 107 pathway.

**Explanation:** A Part 107 waiver permits deviating from specific Part 107 rules. A COA is an authorization typically used for public aircraft or certain Part 91/public safety operations, not the normal Part 107 pathway.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** They are not interchangeable — they serve different regulatory purposes.
- **C:** Waivers are for civilian Part 107 operators who need rule deviation; neither is exclusively military.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.200 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: regulations, waiver, coa, authorization_

---

#### REG-V-012

**Q:** Under current Part 107, when can you fly at night without a waiver?

- **A.** If the RPIC completed the required knowledge test or training after April 6, 2021, and the drone has anti-collision lighting visible for at least 3 statute miles. ✅
- **B.** Night flight is always prohibited without a waiver.
- **C.** Anytime — nighttime restrictions were fully removed in 2021.

**✅ Answer:** A — If the RPIC completed the required knowledge test or training after April 6, 2021, and the drone has anti-collision lighting visible for at least 3 statute miles.

**Explanation:** Night operations are allowed under current Part 107 if the RPIC completed the required initial knowledge test or training after April 6, 2021, and the drone is equipped with anti-collision lighting visible for at least 3 statute miles.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Night waivers are no longer required if the pilot meets the current training requirement.
- **C:** Lighting and training requirements still apply — night flight is not completely unrestricted.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K3 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.29 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: regulations, night, operations_

---

#### REG-V-013

**Q:** Under Part 107, when are operations over people allowed without a waiver?

- **A.** Only if the person is directly participating, under a covered structure, inside a stationary vehicle, or the operation meets Category 1, 2, 3, or 4 requirements. ✅
- **B.** Operations over people are always allowed below 400 ft AGL.
- **C.** Operations over people require a waiver in all cases.

**✅ Answer:** A — Only if the person is directly participating, under a covered structure, inside a stationary vehicle, or the operation meets Category 1, 2, 3, or 4 requirements.

**Explanation:** Part 107 allows operations over people only for direct participants, those under covered structures, those in stationary vehicles, or when the operation fits Category 1, 2, 3, or 4. Category 3 cannot be used for open-air assemblies.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Altitude alone does not authorize operations over non-participating people.
- **C:** Categories 1–4 provide waiver-free pathways for operations over people.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K3 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.110–107.145 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: regulations, operations-over-people, categories_

---

#### REG-V-014

**Q:** What is the maximum altitude under Part 107, and when does the 400 ft limit not apply?

- **A.** Maximum is 400 ft AGL, unless within 400 ft of a structure — then you may fly up to 400 ft above that structure's uppermost limit. ✅
- **B.** Maximum is 400 ft AGL with no exceptions.
- **C.** Maximum is 500 ft AGL unless in controlled airspace.

**✅ Answer:** A — Maximum is 400 ft AGL, unless within 400 ft of a structure — then you may fly up to 400 ft above that structure's uppermost limit.

**Explanation:** The default max altitude is 400 ft AGL. When operating within 400 ft of a structure, the drone may fly up to 400 ft above the structure's immediate uppermost limit.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The structure exception is a real and important exception to the 400 ft AGL rule.
- **C:** The limit is 400 ft AGL, not 500 ft.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K3 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.51 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: regulations, altitude, 400-ft-rule_

---

#### REG-V-017

**Q:** Under Part 107, what right-of-way rule applies to small unmanned aircraft?

- **A.** Small unmanned aircraft must yield right of way to all aircraft, airborne vehicles, and launch and reentry vehicles. ✅
- **B.** Manned aircraft must yield to drones in controlled airspace.
- **C.** Right of way is determined by who is operating at lower altitude.

**✅ Answer:** A — Small unmanned aircraft must yield right of way to all aircraft, airborne vehicles, and launch and reentry vehicles.

**Explanation:** Under Part 107, small unmanned aircraft must yield right of way to all aircraft, airborne vehicles, and launch and reentry vehicles.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Drones always yield to manned aircraft, not the other way around.
- **C:** Altitude does not determine right of way — drones always yield.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K3 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.37 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: regulations, right-of-way, safety_

#### REG-V-018

**Q:** What preflight familiarization and inspection is required before a Part 107 flight?

- **A.** Assess local weather, airspace and flight restrictions, nearby people and property, other ground hazards, and verify the aircraft is in a condition for safe operation. ✅
- **B.** Only verify the aircraft battery is charged.
- **C.** No preflight is required for flights under 10 minutes.

**✅ Answer:** A — Assess local weather, airspace and flight restrictions, nearby people and property, other ground hazards, and verify the aircraft is in a condition for safe operation.

**Explanation:** Before flight, the RPIC must assess local weather, airspace and flight restrictions, nearby people and property, other ground hazards, and verify the aircraft is in a condition for safe operation.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Preflight includes airspace, weather, and safety assessment — not just battery check.
- **C:** Preflight requirements apply to every flight regardless of duration.

_ACS: UA.V.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.49 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: regulations, preflight, inspection_

---

#### REG-V-019

**Q:** When must a drone accident be reported to the FAA under Part 107?

- **A.** Within 10 calendar days if the operation causes serious injury, loss of consciousness, or more than $500 damage to property other than the drone. ✅
- **B.** Within 24 hours for any crash landing.
- **C.** Only if an manned aircraft is involved.

**✅ Answer:** A — Within 10 calendar days if the operation causes serious injury, loss of consciousness, or more than $500 damage to property other than the drone.

**Explanation:** Report to the FAA within 10 calendar days if the operation causes serious injury, loss of consciousness, or more than $500 damage to property other than the drone.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The threshold is serious injury or over $500 property damage — not every crash landing.
- **C:** The reporting requirement is not limited to incidents involving manned aircraft.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K5 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.9 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: regulations, accident-reporting, safety_

---

#### REG-V-020

**Q:** What alcohol rule applies to Part 107 pilots and Visual Observers?

- **A.** Must comply with 14 CFR 91.17 and 91.19: 8-hour bottle-to-throttle rule and no alcohol concentration of 0.04 or greater. ✅
- **B.** No numeric alcohol limit — just no visible impairment.
- **C.** The 8-hour rule applies to the RPIC only, not the VO.

**✅ Answer:** A — Must comply with 14 CFR 91.17 and 91.19: 8-hour bottle-to-throttle rule and no alcohol concentration of 0.04 or greater.

**Explanation:** Part 107 cross-references 14 CFR 91.17 and 91.19, requiring the 8-hour bottle-to-throttle rule and no blood alcohol concentration of 0.04 or greater. This applies to the RPIC, person manipulating controls, and VO.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** There is a numeric limit of 0.04 blood alcohol concentration.
- **C:** The alcohol rule applies to all crew, including VOs.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K5 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.57; 91.17; 91.19 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: regulations, alcohol, safety_

#### REG-V-024

**Q:** What is the format of the UAG Remote Pilot knowledge test?

- **A.** 60 questions, 2.0 hours allowed, 70 percent passing score. ✅
- **B.** 80 questions, 3.0 hours allowed, 75 percent passing score.
- **C.** 40 questions, 1.0 hour allowed, 70 percent passing score.

**✅ Answer:** A — 60 questions, 2.0 hours allowed, 70 percent passing score.

**Explanation:** The UAG Remote Pilot knowledge test has 60 questions, a 2.0-hour time limit, and a 70 percent passing score, as listed in the current FAA testing matrix.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The test has 60 questions, not 80, and 2.0 hours, not 3.0.
- **C:** The test has 60 questions and 2.0 hours, not 40 questions and 1.0 hour.

_ACS: UA.I.A.K1 | Citation: FAA UAG Knowledge Test Matrix | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: regulations, exam, test-format_

## Radio Communications

**65 questions**

### Acronyms & Abbreviations

#### ACR-020

**Q:** What does "CTAF" stand for?

- **A.** Common Traffic Advisory Frequency. ✅
- **B.** Controlled Tower Approach Frequency.
- **C.** Central Terminal Area Frequency.

**✅ Answer:** A — Common Traffic Advisory Frequency.

**Explanation:** CTAF stands for Common Traffic Advisory Frequency — used for airport advisory practices at non-towered airports. The CTAF may be a UNICOM, MULTICOM, FSS, or tower frequency.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** CTAF is not a controlled tower frequency. It is the Common Traffic Advisory Frequency.
- **C:** CTAF does not refer to a terminal area. It is used for traffic advisories at non-towered airports.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: AIM §4-1-9 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, ctaf, communications_

---

#### ACR-031

**Q:** What does "FS" stand for in FAA services?

- **A.** Flight Service. ✅
- **B.** Federal Security.
- **C.** Fuel Station.

**✅ Answer:** A — Flight Service.

**Explanation:** FS stands for Flight Service — providing pilot briefings, weather, NOTAM processing, flight plan services, and aeronautical information.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** FS does not mean Federal Security. It is Flight Service.
- **C:** FS is not related to fuel. It refers to the Flight Service system.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: AIM §7-1-2 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, fs, flight-service_

---

#### ACR-038

**Q:** What does "LAA" stand for?

- **A.** Local Airport Advisory. ✅
- **B.** Low Altitude Approach.
- **C.** Landing Area Authorization.

**✅ Answer:** A — Local Airport Advisory.

**Explanation:** LAA stands for Local Airport Advisory — a service provided by facilities at airports not served by a control tower, giving pilots information about wind, runway, and traffic.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** LAA is not a type of approach. It is a local advisory service.
- **C:** LAA does not refer to authorization. It is a Local Airport Advisory service.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-5-1 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, laa, advisory_

---

#### ACR-064

**Q:** What does "UNICOM" stand for?

- **A.** Aeronautical Advisory Communications Stations. ✅
- **B.** Universal Communications Module.
- **C.** Unified Navigation and Information Command.

**✅ Answer:** A — Aeronautical Advisory Communications Stations.

**Explanation:** UNICOM stands for Aeronautical Advisory Communications Stations — nongovernment facilities that may provide airport information at certain airports. Frequencies are shown on charts.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** UNICOM is not a communications module. It is a nongovernment advisory facility.
- **C:** UNICOM is not a navigation command. It provides airport advisory information.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: AIM §4-1-11 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, unicom, communications_

### Extended Terms

#### EXT-008

**Q:** What is a CTAF?

- **A.** Common Traffic Advisory Frequency — used for airport advisory practices at airports without an operating control tower. ✅
- **B.** Control Tower Approach Frequency — assigned by ATC for instrument approaches.
- **C.** Commercial Traffic Announcement Frequency for airline operations.

**✅ Answer:** A — Common Traffic Advisory Frequency — used for airport advisory practices at airports without an operating control tower.

**Explanation:** CTAF (Common Traffic Advisory Frequency) is used at airports without an operating control tower. The CTAF may be a UNICOM, MULTICOM, FSS, or tower frequency.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Instrument approach frequencies are assigned by ATC, not called CTAF.
- **C:** CTAF is not commercial-specific; it is for any aircraft at non-towered airports.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-1-9 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: radio, ctaf, communications_

---

#### EXT-009

**Q:** What is UNICOM?

- **A.** A nongovernment communication facility that may provide airport information at certain airports. Locations and frequencies are shown on aeronautical charts. ✅
- **B.** A universal emergency communication system for all aircraft.
- **C.** The ATC tower frequency at Class D airports.

**✅ Answer:** A — A nongovernment communication facility that may provide airport information at certain airports. Locations and frequencies are shown on aeronautical charts.

**Explanation:** UNICOM is a nongovernment communication facility that may provide airport information at certain airports. Its locations and frequencies are shown on aeronautical charts and publications.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Emergency communications use Guard frequency (121.5 MHz), not UNICOM.
- **C:** Class D airports have an ATC tower with an assigned frequency, not UNICOM.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-1-11 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: radio, unicom, communications_

---

#### EXT-010

**Q:** What is MULTICOM?

- **A.** A mobile service for communications essential to private aircraft activities — commonly used for self-announce where no tower or UNICOM is available. ✅
- **B.** A multi-channel ATC communication system.
- **C.** A frequency used only for drone operators near uncontrolled airports.

**✅ Answer:** A — A mobile service for communications essential to private aircraft activities — commonly used for self-announce where no tower or UNICOM is available.

**Explanation:** MULTICOM is a mobile service used for communications essential to private aircraft activities. In pilot training, it is the self-announce option where no tower or UNICOM is available.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MULTICOM is a designated frequency, not an ATC system.
- **C:** MULTICOM is used by all general aviation, not exclusively by drone operators.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-1-11 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: radio, multicom, communications_

---

#### EXT-011

**Q:** What is ATIS?

- **A.** Automatic Terminal Information Service — continuous broadcast of recorded noncontrol information in selected high-activity terminal areas. ✅
- **B.** Air Traffic Information System — the computer system tracking radar targets.
- **C.** Advanced Tower Instruction Sequence used at busy Class B airports.

**✅ Answer:** A — Automatic Terminal Information Service — continuous broadcast of recorded noncontrol information in selected high-activity terminal areas.

**Explanation:** ATIS is the Automatic Terminal Information Service, providing a continuous broadcast of recorded noncontrol information such as weather, NOTAMs, and active runways at high-activity terminal areas.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Radar tracking systems have different names; ATIS is an audio broadcast.
- **C:** ATIS is a passive information broadcast, not a sequencing system.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-1-13 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: radio, atis, communications_

---

#### EXT-012

**Q:** What is an FSS?

- **A.** Flight Service Station — provides pilot briefings, flight plan processing, search and rescue help, weather, NOTAM processing, and aeronautical information. ✅
- **B.** Federal Surveillance System — radar monitoring network.
- **C.** Flight Safety System — the black box recorder on manned aircraft.

**✅ Answer:** A — Flight Service Station — provides pilot briefings, flight plan processing, search and rescue help, weather, NOTAM processing, and aeronautical information.

**Explanation:** FSS (Flight Service Station) is an air traffic facility that provides pilot briefings, flight plan processing, search and rescue assistance, weather, NOTAM processing, and aeronautical information.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** FSS is a human-staffed information service, not a radar system.
- **C:** Black box recorders are flight data recorders (FDR), not FSS.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-1-3 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: radio, fss, communications_

### Phonetic Alphabet

#### PA-001

**Q:** In the ICAO/NATO phonetic alphabet used in aviation radio communications, what is the phonetic word for the letter "A"?

- **A.** Alfa ✅
- **B.** Alpha
- **C.** Actual

**✅ Answer:** A — Alfa

**Explanation:** "Alfa" (pronounced AL FAH) is the ICAO phonetic word for the letter "A". It is used worldwide in aviation radio communications to eliminate confusion between similar-sounding letters.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** "Alpha" is not the ICAO standard phonetic word for "A". The correct word is "Alfa".
- **C:** "Actual" is not the ICAO standard phonetic word for "A". The correct word is "Alfa".

**Explanation:** "Victor" is the ICAO phonetic word for the letter "V". Phonetic alphabet words are used in radio communications so each letter is clearly understood despite noise or interference.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The letter "U" is not represented by "Victor". "U" is represented by a different phonetic word.
- **C:** The letter "W" is not represented by "Victor". "W" is represented by a different phonetic word.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: ICAO Annex 10 / FAA AIM Chapter 4 | Source: ICAO Phonetic Alphabet (Skybrary) | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: phonetic-alphabet, radio, icao, reverse_

---

#### PA-049

**Q:** An ATC controller clears you to operate in the vicinity of airport "KBOS." When reading back "B" over the radio, what phonetic word do you use?

- **A.** Bravo ✅
- **B.** Baker
- **C.** Beta

**✅ Answer:** A — Bravo

**Explanation:** "Bravo" (pronounced BRAH VOH) is the ICAO phonetic word for "B". Using standard phonetics eliminates radio communication errors, especially in noisy environments.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** "Baker" was used in the older US military phonetic alphabet but is NOT the current ICAO standard. The correct word is "Bravo."
- **C:** "Beta" is not an ICAO phonetic word. The correct word for "B" is "Bravo."

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: ICAO Annex 10 / FAA AIM Chapter 4 | Source: ICAO Phonetic Alphabet (Skybrary) | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: phonetic-alphabet, radio, icao, applied_

---

#### PA-050

**Q:** A controller assigns squawk code 4271. You read back "4271" as: "Four Two Seven One." What is the ICAO-correct way to say the digit "9" if it appeared in this code?

- **A.** Niner (NIN ER) ✅
- **B.** Nine (standard pronunciation)
- **C.** November

**✅ Answer:** A — Niner (NIN ER)

**Explanation:** "Niner" (NIN ER) is the ICAO standard phonetic for the digit "9". The altered pronunciation prevents confusion with the German word "nein" (meaning "no") during international communications.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** "Nine" using standard English pronunciation is NOT the ICAO standard. ICAO specifies "Niner" (NIN ER) to prevent international misunderstanding.
- **C:** "November" is the phonetic for the letter "N", not the digit "9". The digit "9" is "Niner".

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: ICAO Annex 10 / FAA AIM Chapter 4 | Source: ICAO Phonetic Alphabet (Skybrary) | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: phonetic-alphabet, radio, icao, applied_

---

#### PA-051

**Q:** Why does the ICAO phonetic alphabet say "Alfa" instead of "Alpha" and "Juliett" instead of "Juliet"?

- **A.** To ensure correct pronunciation by non-English speakers worldwide ✅
- **B.** Because the FAA prefers older English spellings
- **C.** To distinguish them from common first names

**✅ Answer:** A — To ensure correct pronunciation by non-English speakers worldwide

**Explanation:** ICAO phonetic spellings like "Alfa" and "Juliett" are designed so the words are pronounced consistently by speakers of all languages. "Alpha" in French is read differently, so "Alfa" standardizes the pronunciation globally.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The FAA follows ICAO international standards. The altered spellings are for global linguistic consistency, not stylistic preference.
- **C:** While incidental, the actual reason is international pronunciation standardization. "Alfa" ensures non-English speakers pronounce it correctly.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: ICAO Annex 10 / FAA AIM Chapter 4 | Source: ICAO Phonetic Alphabet (Skybrary) | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: phonetic-alphabet, radio, icao, applied_

---

#### PA-052

**Q:** What is the phonetic way to say the number "500" over the radio?

- **A.** Fife Hundred ✅
- **B.** Five Zero Zero
- **C.** Five Hundred

**✅ Answer:** A — Fife Hundred

**Explanation:** "Fife Hundred" — digits are spoken individually using their phonetic pronunciations ("Fife" for 5), and "Hundred" is pronounced "HUN DRED". So 500 = "Fife Hundred".

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** "Five Zero Zero" uses standard English rather than ICAO phonetic pronunciation. ICAO uses "Fife" for 5, not "Five".
- **C:** "Five Hundred" uses standard English. ICAO requires "Fife Hundred" with the specialized digit pronunciation.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: ICAO Annex 10 / FAA AIM Chapter 4 | Source: ICAO Phonetic Alphabet (Skybrary) | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: phonetic-alphabet, radio, icao, applied_

---

#### PA-053

**Q:** A NOTAM states an airspace restriction in "Class Bravo." Which letter does "Bravo" represent?

- **A.** B ✅
- **B.** D
- **C.** P

**✅ Answer:** A — B

**Explanation:** "Bravo" is the ICAO phonetic word for the letter "B". Class B (Bravo) airspace surrounds the busiest airports and requires ATC authorization before entering.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** "Delta" is the phonetic for "D". Class D airspace surrounds smaller airports with control towers.
- **C:** "Papa" is the phonetic for "P". Class P is Prohibited airspace.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: ICAO Annex 10 / FAA AIM Chapter 4 | Source: ICAO Phonetic Alphabet (Skybrary) | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: phonetic-alphabet, radio, icao, applied_

---

#### PA-054

**Q:** What does "Mayday Mayday Mayday" indicate in radio communications?

- **A.** A life-threatening emergency requiring immediate assistance ✅
- **B.** An urgency condition requiring priority handling
- **C.** A request to change radio frequency

**✅ Answer:** A — A life-threatening emergency requiring immediate assistance

**Explanation:** "Mayday" (from French m'aidez, "help me") repeated three times is the international distress call for a life-threatening emergency. For a less critical urgency, the phrase "Pan-Pan" is used instead.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** "Pan-Pan" (repeated three times) indicates an urgency condition. "Mayday" is reserved for life-threatening emergencies only.
- **C:** Changing frequencies uses phrases like "switching to [frequency]" — not a distress call.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: ICAO Annex 10 / FAA AIM Chapter 4 | Source: ICAO Phonetic Alphabet (Skybrary) | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: phonetic-alphabet, radio, icao, applied_

---

#### PA-055

**Q:** When a controller says "N12345, taxi to runway two-seven," what does "two-seven" mean?

- **A.** Runway 27 — oriented approximately 270° magnetic ✅
- **B.** Runway 27 — the 27th runway on the airport
- **C.** Taxi to a parking spot numbered 27

**✅ Answer:** A — Runway 27 — oriented approximately 270° magnetic

**Explanation:** Runway numbers represent the magnetic heading divided by 10. "Two-seven" = Runway 27 = approximately 270° magnetic (due West). Controllers use spoken numbers for each digit to avoid misunderstanding.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Runway numbers are NOT sequential IDs. They represent the approximate magnetic heading for takeoff/landing. Runway 27 ≈ 270°.
- **C:** Runway numbers indicate the direction of flight, not a parking spot number. "Taxi to runway two-seven" means runway 27.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: ICAO Annex 10 / FAA AIM Chapter 4 | Source: ICAO Phonetic Alphabet (Skybrary) | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: phonetic-alphabet, radio, icao, applied_

---

#### PA-056

**Q:** What is the correct phonetic pronunciation of the letter "C" per ICAO standards?

- **A.** Charlie (CHAR LEE) ✅
- **B.** Cuba (KOO BAH)
- **C.** Cobra (KOH BRAH)

**✅ Answer:** A — Charlie (CHAR LEE)

**Explanation:** "Charlie" (pronounced CHAR LEE or SHAR LEE) is the ICAO phonetic word for "C". It is universally recognized in aviation worldwide.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** "Cuba" is not an ICAO phonetic word. The correct word for "C" is "Charlie".
- **C:** "Cobra" is not an ICAO phonetic word. The correct word for "C" is "Charlie".

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: ICAO Annex 10 / FAA AIM Chapter 4 | Source: ICAO Phonetic Alphabet (Skybrary) | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: phonetic-alphabet, radio, icao, applied_
