## Airspace

**64 questions**

### Acronyms & Abbreviations

#### ACR-005

**Q:** What does "ADIZ" stand for?

- **A.** Air Defense Identification Zone. ✅
- **B.** Airport Departure Instrument Zone.
- **C.** Automated Drone Identification Zone.

**✅ Answer:** A — Air Defense Identification Zone.

**Explanation:** ADIZ stands for Air Defense Identification Zone — airspace over land or water where the identification, location, and control of aircraft is required in the interest of national security.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** ADIZ has nothing to do with instrument departures. It is a national security airspace zone.
- **C:** ADIZ predates drone regulations. It is an Air Defense Identification Zone.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §99 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, adiz, airspace_

---

#### ACR-009

**Q:** What does "AGL" stand for?

- **A.** Above Ground Level. ✅
- **B.** Approved Guidance Limit.
- **C.** Average Geographic Latitude.

**✅ Answer:** A — Above Ground Level.

**Explanation:** AGL stands for Above Ground Level — height measured from the surface directly below the aircraft. Part 107's 400 ft altitude limit is measured in AGL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** AGL does not refer to guidance limits. It is a measurement of height above the ground.
- **C:** AGL has nothing to do with latitude. It means Above Ground Level.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.51(b) | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, agl, altitude_

---

#### ACR-012

**Q:** What does "ATC" stand for?

- **A.** Air Traffic Control. ✅
- **B.** Aircraft Technical Certificate.
- **C.** Aviation Training Center.

**✅ Answer:** A — Air Traffic Control.

**Explanation:** ATC stands for Air Traffic Control — the service operated by the FAA to promote the safe and efficient flow of air traffic. Part 107 requires ATC authorization for controlled airspace operations.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** ATC does not refer to a certificate. It is the Air Traffic Control service.
- **C:** ATC is not a training center. It is the Air Traffic Control system.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.41 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, atc, airspace_

---

#### ACR-015

**Q:** What does "CFA" stand for?

- **A.** Controlled Firing Areas. ✅
- **B.** Center Frequency Assignment.
- **C.** Commercial Flight Authorization.

**✅ Answer:** A — Controlled Firing Areas.

**Explanation:** CFA stands for Controlled Firing Areas — airspace where activities such as weapons testing are conducted under conditions that ensure the safety of non-participating aircraft. CFAs are not charted because activities are suspended when aircraft are detected.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** CFA is not related to frequency assignments. It designates areas with controlled weapons firing.
- **C:** CFA does not refer to flight authorization. It is a type of special use airspace.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-4-4 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, cfa, special-use-airspace_

---

#### ACR-023

**Q:** What does "DVFR" stand for?

- **A.** Defense VFR. ✅
- **B.** Direct Visual Flight Route.
- **C.** Designated VFR.

**✅ Answer:** A — Defense VFR.

**Explanation:** DVFR stands for Defense VFR — a VFR flight plan that must be filed for flights operating within an Air Defense Identification Zone (ADIZ).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** DVFR is not a flight route. It is a Defense VFR flight plan required in an ADIZ.
- **C:** DVFR does not mean Designated VFR. The D stands for Defense.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §99.11 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, dvfr, defense_

---

#### ACR-028

**Q:** What does "FDC" stand for in the context of NOTAMs?

- **A.** Flight Data Center. ✅
- **B.** Federal Drone Command.
- **C.** Flight Dispatch Control.

**✅ Answer:** A — Flight Data Center.

**Explanation:** FDC stands for Flight Data Center — a regulatory NOTAM type. Temporary Flight Restrictions (TFRs) are issued as FDC NOTAMs.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** FDC is not a drone command. FDC NOTAMs are regulatory notices from the Flight Data Center.
- **C:** FDC does not mean Flight Dispatch Control. It is the source of regulatory NOTAMs.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §5-1-3 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, fdc, notam_

---

#### ACR-029

**Q:** What does "FL" stand for?

- **A.** Flight Level. ✅
- **B.** Final Leg.
- **C.** Fuel Limit.

**✅ Answer:** A — Flight Level.

**Explanation:** FL stands for Flight Level — a standard nominal altitude expressed in hundreds of feet (e.g., FL180 = 18,000 feet MSL). Flight levels begin at 18,000 feet MSL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** FL does not mean Final Leg. It designates altitude levels above 18,000 feet.
- **C:** FL is not related to fuel. It is a pressure altitude reference.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-1-1 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, fl, altitude_

---

#### ACR-030

**Q:** What does "FRZ" stand for?

- **A.** Flight Restriction Zone. ✅
- **B.** Federal Radar Zone.
- **C.** Fuel Reserve Zone.

**✅ Answer:** A — Flight Restriction Zone.

**Explanation:** FRZ stands for Flight Restriction Zone — a defined area of airspace (most notably around Washington, D.C.) where flight is restricted or prohibited.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** FRZ does not refer to radar. It is a Flight Restriction Zone.
- **C:** FRZ has nothing to do with fuel. It restricts flight in certain airspace.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §93 Subpart V | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, frz, restricted-airspace_

---

#### ACR-033

**Q:** What does "IAP" stand for?

- **A.** Instrument Approach Procedures. ✅
- **B.** Initial Approach Point.
- **C.** International Airport Protocol.

**✅ Answer:** A — Instrument Approach Procedures.

**Explanation:** IAP stands for Instrument Approach Procedures — series of predetermined maneuvers for the orderly transfer of an aircraft under instrument flight conditions.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** While 'Initial Approach Point' is a concept, IAP officially stands for Instrument Approach Procedures.
- **C:** IAP is not an international protocol. It refers to instrument landing procedures.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §5-4-1 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, iap, instrument_

---

#### ACR-035

**Q:** What does "IFR" stand for?

- **A.** Instrument Flight Rules. ✅
- **B.** Initial Flight Restriction.
- **C.** International Flight Regulations.

**✅ Answer:** A — Instrument Flight Rules.

**Explanation:** IFR stands for Instrument Flight Rules — the rules governing flight when visual reference to the ground is not possible. Part 107 operations are conducted under VFR, not IFR.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** IFR is not about restrictions. It is a set of flight rules for low-visibility conditions.
- **C:** IFR is not international. It is the Instrument Flight Rules system.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §91 Subpart C | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, ifr, flight-rules_

---

#### ACR-036

**Q:** On sectional charts, what does "IR" stand for?

- **A.** Instrument Routes. ✅
- **B.** Inactive Runway.
- **C.** Instrument Restriction.

**✅ Answer:** A — Instrument Routes.

**Explanation:** IR stands for Instrument Routes — military training routes flown under IFR. On sectional charts, IR routes with four-digit numbers are flown at or below 1,500 ft AGL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** IR does not mean Inactive Runway. It labels Instrument Routes for military training.
- **C:** IR is not an instrument restriction. It identifies specific military training routes.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-5-2 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, ir, military-routes, sectional_

---

#### ACR-039

**Q:** What does "MAP" stand for in instrument procedures?

- **A.** Missed Approach Point. ✅
- **B.** Maximum Altitude Point.
- **C.** Military Approach Pattern.

**✅ Answer:** A — Missed Approach Point.

**Explanation:** MAP stands for Missed Approach Point — the point in an instrument approach at which the pilot must execute a missed approach if the runway is not in sight.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MAP does not refer to a maximum altitude. It is the decision point on an instrument approach.
- **C:** MAP is not a military pattern. It is the Missed Approach Point.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §5-4-5 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, map, instrument_

---

#### ACR-040

**Q:** What does "MDA" stand for?

- **A.** Minimum Descent Altitude. ✅
- **B.** Maximum Drone Altitude.
- **C.** Military Defense Area.

**✅ Answer:** A — Minimum Descent Altitude.

**Explanation:** MDA stands for Minimum Descent Altitude — the lowest altitude to which descent is authorized on a non-precision instrument approach until the pilot sees the required visual references.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MDA is not related to drones. It is a non-precision instrument approach altitude.
- **C:** MDA does not mean Military Defense Area. It is the Minimum Descent Altitude.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §5-4-5 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, mda, instrument_

---

#### ACR-043

**Q:** What does "MOA" stand for?

- **A.** Military Operations Area. ✅
- **B.** Mandatory Operating Altitude.
- **C.** Maximum Operating Area.

**✅ Answer:** A — Military Operations Area.

**Explanation:** MOA stands for Military Operations Area — airspace established outside Class A to separate certain nonhazardous military activities from IFR traffic and identify the area for VFR pilots.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MOA is not an altitude requirement. It is a type of special use airspace.
- **C:** MOA does not mean Maximum Operating Area. It designates military training airspace.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-4-5 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, moa, special-use-airspace_

---

#### ACR-044

**Q:** What does "MSL" stand for?

- **A.** Mean Sea Level. ✅
- **B.** Maximum Safety Limit.
- **C.** Minimum Separation Lateral.

**✅ Answer:** A — Mean Sea Level.

**Explanation:** MSL stands for Mean Sea Level — many charted altitudes, airspace floors, and ceilings are expressed as feet above MSL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MSL is not a safety limit. It is the reference datum for altitude measurement.
- **C:** MSL has nothing to do with lateral separation. It means Mean Sea Level.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-1-1 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, msl, altitude_

---

#### ACR-045

**Q:** What does "MTR" stand for?

- **A.** Military Training Route. ✅
- **B.** Maximum Transition Rate.
- **C.** Minimum Terrain Restriction.

**✅ Answer:** A — Military Training Route.

**Explanation:** MTR stands for Military Training Route — routes used for military training at airspeeds greater than 250 knots. Routes with 4-digit numbers are flown at or below 1,500 ft AGL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MTR is not a transition rate. It identifies routes for high-speed military training flights.
- **C:** MTR has nothing to do with terrain restrictions. It designates Military Training Routes.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-5-2 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, mtr, military-routes_

---

#### ACR-048

**Q:** What does "NAS" stand for?

- **A.** National Airspace System. ✅
- **B.** Non-Authorized Space.
- **C.** North American Sector.

**✅ Answer:** A — National Airspace System.

**Explanation:** NAS stands for National Airspace System — the common network of U.S. airspace including air navigation facilities, equipment, services, airports, and rules.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** NAS does not mean non-authorized space. It encompasses all U.S. airspace and supporting infrastructure.
- **C:** NAS is not a geographic sector. It is the National Airspace System.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM Glossary | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, nas, airspace_

---

#### ACR-051

**Q:** What does "NOTAM" stand for according to current FAA terminology (2025)?

- **A.** Notice to Airmen. ✅
- **B.** Notice to Air Missions.
- **C.** National Operations and Traffic Advisory Message.

**✅ Answer:** A — Notice to Airmen.

**Explanation:** NOTAM stands for Notice to Airmen. FAA Order JO 7930.2U changed the term back to 'Notice to Airmen' effective 2025.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 'Notice to Air Missions' was briefly used but the current FAA order reverted to Notice to Airmen.
- **C:** NOTAM does not stand for any version with 'Traffic Advisory Message.'

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: FAA Order JO 7930.2U | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, notam, current-terminology_

---

#### ACR-052

**Q:** What does "NSA" stand for in aviation charting?

- **A.** National Security Area. ✅
- **B.** No-fly Safety Area.
- **C.** Non-Standard Airspace.

**✅ Answer:** A — National Security Area.

**Explanation:** NSA stands for National Security Area — airspace where there is a requirement for increased security of ground facilities. Flight in NSAs is voluntarily avoided.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** NSA is not labeled a 'no-fly' area. Pilots are requested but not required to avoid them.
- **C:** NSA does not mean non-standard airspace. It is a National Security Area.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-4-8 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, nsa, security_

---

#### ACR-056

**Q:** What does "SAO" stand for?

- **A.** Special Area of Operation. ✅
- **B.** Standard Approach Overlay.
- **C.** Surveillance and Observation.

**✅ Answer:** A — Special Area of Operation.

**Explanation:** SAO stands for Special Area of Operation — a designated area where certain operations or activities are conducted under specific conditions.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** SAO is not an approach overlay. It designates special operational areas.
- **C:** SAO does not refer to surveillance. It is a Special Area of Operation.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM Glossary | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, sao, operations_

---

#### ACR-060

**Q:** What does "TFR" stand for?

- **A.** Temporary Flight Restriction. ✅
- **B.** Terminal Frequency Range.
- **C.** Traffic Flow Regulation.

**✅ Answer:** A — Temporary Flight Restriction.

**Explanation:** TFR stands for Temporary Flight Restriction — a temporary airspace restriction commonly issued for security, VIP movement, disasters, major events, or safety. TFRs are issued as FDC NOTAMs.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** TFR is not related to frequencies. It restricts flight in a defined area temporarily.
- **C:** TFR does not regulate traffic flow. It temporarily restricts flight in specific airspace.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §91.137-139 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, tfr, notam, restrictions_

---

#### ACR-062

**Q:** What does "TRSA" stand for?

- **A.** Terminal Radar Service Area. ✅
- **B.** Temporary Restricted Safety Area.
- **C.** Traffic Routing and Separation Area.

**✅ Answer:** A — Terminal Radar Service Area.

**Explanation:** TRSA stands for Terminal Radar Service Area — airspace surrounding certain airports where ATC provides radar services on a voluntary basis. Participation is not mandatory.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** TRSA is not a restricted area. Participation in TRSA services is voluntary.
- **C:** TRSA does not regulate routing. It is a voluntary radar service area.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-5-5 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, trsa, radar_

---

#### ACR-066

**Q:** What does "VFR" stand for?

- **A.** Visual Flight Rules. ✅
- **B.** Variable Frequency Range.
- **C.** Verified Flight Route.

**✅ Answer:** A — Visual Flight Rules.

**Explanation:** VFR stands for Visual Flight Rules — the rules governing flight when there is sufficient visibility to navigate by visual reference. All Part 107 operations are conducted under VFR weather minimums.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** VFR is not a frequency range. It is a set of flight rules for visual conditions.
- **C:** VFR does not mean a verified route. It is Visual Flight Rules.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §91 Subpart B | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, vfr, flight-rules_

---

#### ACR-067

**Q:** On sectional charts, what does "VR" stand for?

- **A.** Visual Routes (military training routes flown under VFR). ✅
- **B.** Variable Range.
- **C.** VFR Restriction.

**✅ Answer:** A — Visual Routes (military training routes flown under VFR).

**Explanation:** VR stands for Visual Routes — military training routes flown under VFR. Like IR routes, routes with 4-digit numbers have segments at or below 1,500 ft AGL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** VR does not mean Variable Range. It identifies VFR military training routes.
- **C:** VR is not a VFR restriction. It labels specific military training routes.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §3-5-2 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, vr, military-routes, sectional_

---

#### ACR-078

**Q:** What does "LAANC" stand for?

- **A.** Low Altitude Authorization and Notification Capability. ✅
- **B.** Local Airspace Advisory and Navigation Control.
- **C.** Limited Altitude Access for Non-Commercial.

**✅ Answer:** A — Low Altitude Authorization and Notification Capability.

**Explanation:** LAANC stands for Low Altitude Authorization and Notification Capability — the FAA system that automates many controlled-airspace authorization requests near airports through approved UAS Service Suppliers.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** LAANC is not an advisory service. It automates airspace authorization for drone pilots.
- **C:** LAANC is available for all Part 107 pilots, not just non-commercial operations.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: FAA UAS Data Exchange | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, laanc, authorization_

---

### Airport Operations

#### AIR-058

**Q:** An aircraft announces on CTAF that it is on short final for runway 9. In which direction will the aircraft be traveling, and from which direction is it approaching?

- **A.** Traveling west, approaching from the east
- **B.** Traveling east, approaching from the west ✅
- **C.** Traveling north, approaching from the south
- **D.** Traveling south, approaching from the north

**✅ Answer:** B — Traveling east, approaching from the west

**Explanation:** Runway numbers correspond to magnetic heading rounded to the nearest 10 degrees. Runway 9 corresponds to a heading of 090° (east). An aircraft on short final for runway 9 is therefore flying toward the east and approaching from the west.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Runway 27 would be heading west — runway 9 is the opposite direction (east).
- **C:** Runway 36 or 360 would be heading north.
- **D:** Runway 18 would be heading south.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K2 | Citation: AIM 2-3-3 (Traffic Patterns and Pilot Communications) | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: runway-numbers, airport-operations, landing-direction_

---

#### AIR-059

**Q:** When approaching runway hold-short lines from the side with continuous (unbroken) lines, the pilot or operator should do what?

- **A.** Proceed across the lines without stopping — they only apply to manned aircraft
- **B.** Stop and do not cross without clearance from the tower ✅
- **C.** Slow down and cross if no aircraft are on final approach
- **D.** Flash lights to signal intent to cross

**✅ Answer:** B — Stop and do not cross without clearance from the tower

**Explanation:** Runway hold-short markings consist of four yellow lines — two solid (continuous) and two dashed. The solid lines are on the side that requires stopping; you must stop and hold short until cleared by ATC. The dashed side indicates you are exiting the runway.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Hold-short markings apply to all operations on an airport surface.
- **C:** You must have positive ATC clearance to cross a hold-short line — situational awareness alone is not sufficient.
- **D:** Light signals are for communication when radio fails — hold-short lines require ATC verbal clearance.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K3 | Citation: AIM 2-3-5 (Runway Markings and Holding Position Markings) | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: hold-short, runway-markings, airport-surface_

---

#### AIR-060

**Q:** You are operating a small UAS northeast of a nearby airport. A pilot announces on CTAF that they are departing runway 36 using a right-hand traffic pattern. Will the aircraft conflict with your operation?

- **A.** No — runway 36 departs to the south, away from your position
- **B.** No — right traffic keeps the aircraft south and west of the airport
- **C.** Yes — the aircraft departs north then turns right (east), directly over your northeast position ✅
- **D.** Only if your UAS is above 400 ft AGL

**✅ Answer:** C — Yes — the aircraft departs north then turns right (east), directly over your northeast position

**Explanation:** Runway 36 is oriented toward a heading of 360° (north). Departing runway 36 with a right-hand traffic pattern means the aircraft climbs northbound, then turns right (east) on crosswind, then southeast on downwind. This path goes directly over the northeast operating area.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Runway 36 takes off heading north, not south — runway 18 departs to the south.
- **B:** A right turn from runway 36 takes the aircraft east and northeast, not south or west.
- **D:** Traffic pattern altitudes are typically 800–1,000 ft AGL — any UAS operating in the area is a potential conflict regardless of altitude.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K4 | Citation: AIM 4-3-2 (Traffic Pattern Entries and Position Reporting) | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 3/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, runway-36, right-traffic, conflict_

### Airspace Authorization

#### PRAC-007

**Q:** A remote pilot plans to operate a small unmanned aircraft system within Class B airspace. Which of the following is required for the operation?

- **A.** The aircraft must be equipped with ADS-B Out.
- **B.** The remote PIC must obtain ATC authorization before operating in Class B airspace. ✅
- **C.** The aircraft must be equipped with a Mode C transponder.

**✅ Answer:** B — The remote PIC must obtain ATC authorization before operating in Class B airspace.

**Explanation:** Part 107 operations in Class B airspace require prior ATC authorization. Small UAS are not required to carry ADS-B Out or a Mode C transponder for this purpose.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** ADS-B Out is not a standard requirement for Part 107 operations in Class B airspace.
- **C:** A Mode C transponder is not required for a small UAS under Part 107 to obtain authorization.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K16 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.41 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: remote pic, class b, atc authorization, controlled airspace_

---

#### PRAC-017

**Q:** A remote pilot receives LAANC authorization for controlled airspace but launches 15 minutes earlier than the approved start time due to favorable weather. Which statement is correct?

- **A.** The authorization remains valid as long as the altitude limits are followed.
- **B.** The operation is unauthorized during the unapproved time window. ✅
- **C.** Early operations are allowed if ATC is not actively monitoring.

**✅ Answer:** B — The operation is unauthorized during the unapproved time window.

**Explanation:** ATC authorization through LAANC is limited to the approved date, time window, and altitude. Launching before the authorized start time is outside the authorization and is therefore not permitted.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Altitude limits are only one part of the authorization; the approved time window also governs the operation.
- **C:** Authorization requirements do not depend on whether ATC is actively monitoring the flight.

_ACS: UA.I.B.K16 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.41; FAA LAANC Guidance | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: remote pilot, laanc, atc authorization, controlled airspace_

### Airspace Classification

#### AIR-001

**Q:** According to 14 CFR part 107 the remote pilot in command (PIC) of a small unmanned aircraft planning to operate within Class C airspace

- **A.** must use a visual observer.
- **B.** is required to file a flight plan.
- **C.** is required to receive ATC authorization. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — is required to receive ATC authorization.

**Explanation:** Under 14 CFR 107.41, the remote PIC must receive ATC authorization before operating in Class B, C, or D airspace, or within the lateral boundaries of the surface area of Class E airspace designated for an airport.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** A visual observer is not required for Class C operations — ATC authorization is the requirement.
- **B:** Part 107 does not require filing a flight plan. The requirement is prior ATC authorization.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1b | Citation: 14 CFR §107.41 | Source: UAG Q3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: class-c, atc-authorization, controlled-airspace_

---

#### AIR-002

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 23, area 3.) What is the floor of the Savannah Class C airspace at the shelf area (outer circle)?

📊 _Refer to: figure-23_

- **A.** 1,300 feet AGL.
- **B.** 1,300 feet MSL. ✅
- **C.** 1,700 feet MSL.

**✅ Answer:** B — 1,300 feet MSL.

**Explanation:** The outer shelf of the Savannah Class C airspace has a floor of 1,300 feet MSL, as indicated on the sectional chart. Airspace altitudes on sectional charts are always expressed in MSL unless specifically noted as AGL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Airspace dimensions on sectional charts are given in MSL, not AGL.
- **C:** 1,700 feet MSL represents a different airspace boundary, not the outer shelf floor.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1b | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 23 | Source: UAG Q8 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: class-c, airspace-floor, sectional-chart, msl_

---

#### AIR-052

**Q:** Which class of airspace does NOT require authorization from Air Traffic Control for small UAS operations?

- **A.** Class B
- **B.** Class C
- **C.** Class D
- **D.** Class G ✅

**✅ Answer:** D — Class G

**Explanation:** Class G airspace is uncontrolled airspace. No ATC authorization is required for small UAS operations in Class G airspace. All controlled airspace classes (B, C, D, and surface E) require prior ATC authorization via LAANC or a Part 107 waiver.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Class B requires ATC authorization under 14 CFR §107.41.
- **B:** Class C requires ATC authorization under 14 CFR §107.41.
- **C:** Class D requires ATC authorization under 14 CFR §107.41.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.41 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: class-g, uncontrolled-airspace, no-authorization_

#### AIR-053

**Q:** What class of airspace surrounds the nation's busiest airports, such as Atlanta Hartsfield-Jackson?

- **A.** Class C
- **B.** Class D
- **C.** Class B ✅
- **D.** Class E

**✅ Answer:** C — Class B

**Explanation:** Class B airspace surrounds the nation's busiest airports (e.g., Atlanta Hartsfield-Jackson, LAX, O'Hare). It is depicted on sectional charts by solid blue lines and typically extends from the surface to 10,000 ft MSL in a layered structure.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Class C surrounds airports with moderate traffic — depicted by solid magenta circles.
- **B:** Class D surrounds smaller towered airports — depicted by dashed blue circles.
- **D:** Class E is general controlled airspace, not specific to the busiest airports.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1 | Citation: AIM 3-2-3 (Class B Airspace) | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: class-b, major-airports, atlanta_

### Extended Terms

#### EXT-001

**Q:** What does AGL mean in aviation?

- **A.** Above Ground Level — height measured from the surface directly below the aircraft. ✅
- **B.** Altitude Guidance Limit — the ceiling set by ATC.
- **C.** Aeronautical Ground Level — MSL corrected for terrain.

**✅ Answer:** A — Above Ground Level — height measured from the surface directly below the aircraft.

**Explanation:** AGL stands for Above Ground Level. It is height measured from the surface directly below the aircraft — not from sea level.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** AGL is a measurement reference, not a limit set by ATC.
- **C:** AGL measures from the surface below; MSL measures from sea level.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1 | Citation: FAA Pilot/Controller Glossary | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: airspace, altitude, agl_

---

#### EXT-002

**Q:** What does MSL mean in aviation?

- **A.** Mean Sea Level — many charted altitudes and airspace floors/ceilings are expressed in MSL. ✅
- **B.** Minimum Safe Level — the lowest permitted flight altitude.
- **C.** Maximum Speed Limit — maximum airspeed near airports.

**✅ Answer:** A — Mean Sea Level — many charted altitudes and airspace floors/ceilings are expressed in MSL.

**Explanation:** MSL stands for Mean Sea Level. Many charted altitudes and airspace floors and ceilings are expressed as MSL values.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MSL is a reference datum, not a regulatory floor.
- **C:** MSL has nothing to do with airspeed limits.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1 | Citation: FAA Pilot/Controller Glossary | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: airspace, altitude, msl_

---

#### EXT-013

**Q:** What is a MOA?

- **A.** Military Operations Area — airspace outside Class A to separate certain nonhazardous military activities from IFR traffic and identify the area for VFR pilots. ✅
- **B.** Mandatory Operations Airspace requiring ATC clearance.
- **C.** A restricted area where all civilian flight is prohibited.

**✅ Answer:** A — Military Operations Area — airspace outside Class A to separate certain nonhazardous military activities from IFR traffic and identify the area for VFR pilots.

**Explanation:** MOA (Military Operations Area) is airspace established outside Class A to separate certain nonhazardous military activities from IFR traffic and to identify the area for VFR pilots.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MOAs do not require ATC clearance for VFR pilots, though caution is advised.
- **C:** MOAs are advisory for VFR traffic; they are not prohibited areas.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR 73; AIM 3-4-5 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: airspace, moa, military_

---

#### EXT-014

**Q:** What is an MTR?

- **A.** Military Training Route — airspace of defined vertical and lateral dimensions used for military training at airspeeds greater than 250 knots. ✅
- **B.** Mandatory Traffic Route near Class B airports.
- **C.** Metropolitan Terminal Region — a wide-area airspace designation.

**✅ Answer:** A — Military Training Route — airspace of defined vertical and lateral dimensions used for military training at airspeeds greater than 250 knots.

**Explanation:** MTR (Military Training Route) is airspace of defined vertical and lateral dimensions established for military training at airspeeds greater than 250 knots indicated airspeed.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** MTRs are military training routes, not mandatory civilian traffic routes.
- **C:** MTR is a specific airspace type, not a metropolitan designation.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1 | Citation: AIM 3-5-2 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: airspace, mtr, military_

**Q:** When would you use the Sectional Chart Legend on the FAA exam?

- **A.** The legend is the chart symbol cheat sheet. It is worth knowing how to flip to it quickly when a figure has an unfamiliar symbol. ✅
- **B.** The legend is never needed on the exam because all symbols are explained in the question text.
- **C.** The legend is only used for IFR charts, not sectional charts.

**✅ Answer:** A — The legend is the chart symbol cheat sheet. It is worth knowing how to flip to it quickly when a figure has an unfamiliar symbol.

**Explanation:** The sectional chart legend is the symbol cheat sheet used during the exam. Knowing how to quickly reference it for unfamiliar chart symbols can save time on figure-heavy questions.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The exam does include figure questions with symbols that require the legend.
- **C:** Sectional charts are VFR charts; the legend is for those charts specifically.

### NOTAMs

#### AIR-004

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 20, area 5.) How would a remote PIC "CHECK NOTAMS" as noted in the CAUTION box regarding the unmarked balloon?

📊 _Refer to: figure-20_

- **A.** By utilizing the B4UFLY mobile application.
- **B.** By contacting the FAA district office.
- **C.** By obtaining a briefing via an online source such as: 1800WXBrief.com. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — By obtaining a briefing via an online source such as: 1800WXBrief.com.

**Explanation:** NOTAMs (Notices to Air Missions) can be obtained by getting a briefing from Flight Service via 1800WXBrief.com or by calling 1-800-WX-BRIEF. This is the official source for NOTAMs.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** While B4UFLY provides some airspace information, it is not the primary source for NOTAMs. 1800WXBrief.com is the official source.
- **B:** The FAA district office is not the standard resource for obtaining NOTAMs.

### Sectional Charts

#### AIR-054

**Q:** On a sectional chart, what does a magenta vignette (shaded area) around an airport indicate?

- **A.** Class B airspace from the surface
- **B.** Class C airspace from the surface
- **C.** Class E airspace beginning at 700 ft AGL ✅
- **D.** Class G airspace

**✅ Answer:** C — Class E airspace beginning at 700 ft AGL

**Explanation:** A magenta vignette (fading shading) around an airport on a sectional chart indicates Class E airspace that begins at 700 feet AGL. This is the transition area that supports IFR arrivals and departures at the airport.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Class B is depicted by solid blue lines, not a magenta vignette.
- **B:** Class C surface area is depicted by solid magenta circles, not a vignette.
- **D:** Class G is uncontrolled and is not depicted with a vignette.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1 | Citation: AIM 3-2-6 (Class E Airspace) | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: magenta-vignette, class-e, 700-ft, sectional-chart_

---

#### AIR-057

**Q:** On a sectional chart, a gray line with a four-digit route number indicates a Military Training Route (MTR). Could this present a hazard to small UAS operations?

- **A.** No — MTRs only apply to high-altitude operations above 10,000 ft MSL
- **B.** No — military aircraft always coordinate with FAA ATC before using MTRs
- **C.** Yes — MTRs with four-digit numbers are flown at or below 1,500 ft AGL ✅
- **D.** Yes — but only when the route is active during published hours

**✅ Answer:** C — Yes — MTRs with four-digit numbers are flown at or below 1,500 ft AGL

**Explanation:** MTRs with four-digit numbers (e.g., IR1206, VR1001) are conducted at 1,500 ft AGL or below and at speeds exceeding 250 knots. This makes them a direct hazard to sUAS operations at low altitudes. Three-digit MTRs are flown above 1,500 ft AGL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Incorrect — four-digit MTRs specifically operate at or below 1,500 ft AGL.
- **B:** MTR coordination is internal to the military and does not involve real-time ATC advisories for sUAS operators.
- **D:** MTRs can be flown any time and are not restricted to published hours.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K2 | Citation: AIM 3-5-2 (Military Training Routes) | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: mtr, military-training-route, four-digit, 1500-ft_

### Special Use Airspace

#### AIR-003

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 21.) You have been hired by a farmer to use your small UA to inspect his crops. The area that you are to survey is in the Devil's Lake West MOA, east of area 2. How would you find out if the MOA is active?

📊 _Refer to: figure-21_

- **A.** Refer to the chart legend. ✅
- **B.** This information is available in the Small UAS database.
- **C.** Refer to the Military Operations Directory.

**✅ Answer:** A — Refer to the chart legend.

**Explanation:** MOA activity information can be found by referring to the chart legend on the sectional chart, which provides the controlling agency contact information. The specific times of use and contact frequencies are listed in the Chart Supplements U.S. and on the sectional chart border information.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** There is no 'Small UAS database' that provides real-time MOA activity information.
- **C:** There is no publication called the 'Military Operations Directory.' The chart legend and Chart Supplements provide this information.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K2 | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 21; AIM 3-4-5 | Source: UAG Q10 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: moa, special-use-airspace, sectional-chart_

---

#### AIR-005

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 59, area 2.) The chart shows a gray line with "VR1667, VR1617, VR1638, and VR1668." Could this area present a hazard to the operations of a small UA?

📊 _Refer to: figure-59_

- **A.** No, all operations will be above 400 feet.
- **B.** Yes, this is a Military Training Route from the surface to 1,500 feet AGL. ✅
- **C.** Yes, the defined route provides traffic separation to manned aircraft.

**✅ Answer:** B — Yes, this is a Military Training Route from the surface to 1,500 feet AGL.

**Explanation:** VR routes (Visual Routes) with 4-digit numbers are Military Training Routes flown at or below 1,500 feet AGL. Military aircraft travel at high speeds at very low altitudes along these routes, posing a significant hazard to sUAS operations.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Military training routes include operations at low altitudes, well within the altitude range of sUAS operations.
- **C:** Military Training Routes do not provide traffic separation — they are routes where fast-moving military aircraft operate at low altitudes.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K2 | Citation: AIM 3-5-2 (Military Training Routes) | Source: UAG Q30 | Difficulty: 3/5 | Tags: mtr, military-training-route, vr-route, hazard_

---

#### AIR-007

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 26.) The Devils Lake East MOA (area 1) is a

📊 _Refer to: figure-26_

- **A.** meteorological observation area.
- **B.** military observation area.
- **C.** military operations area. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — military operations area.

**Explanation:** MOA stands for Military Operations Area. MOAs are airspace established to separate certain non-hazardous military activities from IFR traffic. Pilots should exercise extreme caution while flying within a MOA.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** MOA does not stand for meteorological observation area.
- **B:** MOA does not stand for military observation area — it is a Military Operations Area.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K2 | Citation: AIM 3-4-5 (Military Operations Areas) | Source: SPA Q36 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: moa, military-operations-area, special-use-airspace_

#### AIR-055

**Q:** What type of special use airspace is designed for high-density student pilot training activity but is NOT classified as controlled or restricted airspace?

- **A.** Warning Area
- **B.** Military Operations Area (MOA)
- **C.** Restricted Area
- **D.** Alert Area ✅

**✅ Answer:** D — Alert Area

**Explanation:** Alert Areas are depicted on aeronautical charts to inform pilots of areas that may contain a high volume of pilot training, unusual aerial activity, or other hazards. They are NOT restricted — you can fly through them but should exercise extreme caution.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Warning Areas contain hazards to nonparticipating aircraft but are located over international waters.
- **B:** MOAs are used for military flight training and may be active or inactive — but are not student training focused.
- **C:** Restricted Areas require ATC authorization and may be active or inactive.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 3-4-6 (Alert Areas) | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: alert-area, special-use-airspace, student-training_

---

#### AIR-056

**Q:** What type of special use airspace designates areas where flight is prohibited due to national security or welfare reasons?

- **A.** Alert Area
- **B.** Restricted Area
- **C.** Military Operations Area (MOA)
- **D.** Prohibited Area ✅

**✅ Answer:** D — Prohibited Area

**Explanation:** Prohibited Areas contain airspace within which flight is prohibited for national security or other welfare reasons. Examples include the airspace over the White House (P-56) and Camp David. Flight in Prohibited Areas is never permitted without specific authorization.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Alert Areas are advisory in nature — they are not restricted or prohibited.
- **B:** Restricted Areas may have hazardous activities but can sometimes be transited with ATC permission.
- **C:** MOAs are used for military training and can often be transited when inactive.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 3-4-2 (Prohibited Areas) | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: prohibited-area, special-use-airspace, national-security_

---

### Temporary Flight Restrictions

#### AIR-006

**Q:** Your surveying company is a title sponsor for a race team at the Indianapolis 500. To promote your new aerial surveying department, you decide to video part of the race using a small UA. The FAA has issued a Temporary Flight Restriction (TFR) for the race in the area you plan to fly. In this situation

- **A.** you may fly your drone in the TFR since your company is sponsoring a team at the race.
- **B.** the TFR applies to all aircraft; you may not fly in the area without a Certificate of Waiver or Authorization. ✅
- **C.** flying your drone is allowed if you notify all non-participating people of the closed course UA operation.

**✅ Answer:** B — the TFR applies to all aircraft; you may not fly in the area without a Certificate of Waiver or Authorization.

**Explanation:** TFRs apply to ALL aircraft, including small unmanned aircraft systems. You may not fly in an active TFR area without a Certificate of Waiver or Authorization (COA) from the FAA. Sponsorship of an event does not grant airspace access.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Sponsoring a team does not grant airspace privileges. TFRs apply to all aircraft regardless of affiliation.
- **C:** Notifying people does not override a TFR. A COA is required.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K3 | Citation: 14 CFR §91.137-145; AIM 3-5-3 | Source: UAG Q46 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: tfr, temporary-flight-restriction, waiver_

#### KUP-047

**Q:** Under 14 CFR Sec. 89.130, what must an operator of a foreign-registered unmanned aircraft do before operating in U.S. airspace?

- **A.** Register the aircraft with the FAA under Part 48
- **B.** Obtain a Confirmation of Identification and keep it at the control station ✅
- **C.** Display the aircraft's registration number on the exterior

**✅ Answer:** B — Obtain a Confirmation of Identification and keep it at the control station

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from 14 CFR Sec. 89.130.

### Verified Rules

#### REG-V-009

**Q:** What is LAANC and how does it work?

- **A.** Low Altitude Authorization and Notification Capability. An FAA/UAS Data Exchange capability that automates many controlled-airspace authorization requests near airports via FAA-approved UAS Service Suppliers. ✅
- **B.** A physical license issued by the FAA that authorizes permanent drone operations near airports.
- **C.** A weather alert system for low-altitude drone pilots.

**✅ Answer:** A — Low Altitude Authorization and Notification Capability. An FAA/UAS Data Exchange capability that automates many controlled-airspace authorization requests near airports via FAA-approved UAS Service Suppliers.

**Explanation:** LAANC automates many controlled-airspace authorization requests near airports through FAA-approved UAS Service Suppliers, enabling near real-time approvals.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** LAANC is an automated digital process, not a physical license.
- **C:** LAANC is an airspace authorization tool, not a weather service.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.41; FAA LAANC Program | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: airspace, laanc, authorization_

---

#### REG-V-010

**Q:** Under Part 107, which airspace classes require prior ATC authorization?

- **A.** Class B, C, D airspace, and within the lateral boundaries of surface Class E designated for an airport. ✅
- **B.** Only Class B airspace requires prior authorization.
- **C.** Class G airspace — all others are unrestricted for drones.

**✅ Answer:** A — Class B, C, D airspace, and within the lateral boundaries of surface Class E designated for an airport.

**Explanation:** Prior ATC authorization is required for Class B, C, D, and within the lateral boundaries of surface Class E designated for an airport.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Class C, D, and surface-designated Class E also require authorization, not only Class B.
- **C:** Class G is uncontrolled and does not require authorization; the other classes do.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.41 | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: airspace, authorization, controlled_

---

#### REG-V-021

**Q:** What is a NOTAM and what does it stand for?

- **A.** Notice to Airmen. Time-critical notices about abnormal changes or hazards in the National Airspace System. ✅
- **B.** Notice to Air Missions. Permanent announcements about airports.
- **C.** National Observation and Tracking Alert Message.

**✅ Answer:** A — Notice to Airmen. Time-critical notices about abnormal changes or hazards in the National Airspace System.

**Explanation:** NOTAM stands for Notice to Airmen. These are time-critical notices about abnormal changes or hazards in the National Airspace System. (FAA Order JO 7930.2U changed the current usage back to Notice to Airmen in 2025.)

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 'Notice to Air Missions' was a previous term. The current FAA order reverted to 'Notice to Airmen' in 2025.
- **C:** That is not a real FAA term.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K2 | Citation: FAA Order JO 7930.2U | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: airspace, notam, definitions_

---

#### REG-V-022

**Q:** What is an FDC NOTAM?

- **A.** A Flight Data Center NOTAM — a regulatory NOTAM type. Temporary Flight Restrictions are issued as FDC NOTAMs. ✅
- **B.** A Federal Defense Command NOTAM issued for military airspace.
- **C.** A local NOTAM issued by the airport tower.

**✅ Answer:** A — A Flight Data Center NOTAM — a regulatory NOTAM type. Temporary Flight Restrictions are issued as FDC NOTAMs.

**Explanation:** FDC stands for Flight Data Center. FDC NOTAMs are regulatory in nature. Temporary Flight Restrictions (TFRs) are issued as FDC NOTAMs.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** FDC stands for Flight Data Center, not Federal Defense Command.
- **C:** FDC NOTAMs are issued nationally, not by local towers.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K2 | Citation: FAA Order JO 7930.2U | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: airspace, notam, fdc, tfr_

---

#### REG-V-023

**Q:** What is a TFR?

- **A.** Temporary Flight Restriction. A temporary airspace restriction commonly issued by FDC NOTAM for security, VIP movement, disasters, major events, or safety. ✅
- **B.** Terminal Flight Route. A published route near an airport.
- **C.** Traffic Flow Restriction issued by the tower controller.

**✅ Answer:** A — Temporary Flight Restriction. A temporary airspace restriction commonly issued by FDC NOTAM for security, VIP movement, disasters, major events, or safety.

**Explanation:** TFR stands for Temporary Flight Restriction, a temporary airspace restriction issued by FDC NOTAM. Common reasons include security, VIP movements, disasters, major events, and safety.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** There is no FAA term 'Terminal Flight Route'.
- **C:** TFRs are issued nationally via FDC NOTAM, not by individual tower controllers.

_ACS: UA.II.A.K2 | Citation: 14 CFR 91.137–91.145; FAA Order JO 7930.2U | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: airspace, tfr, notam_

## Weather

**56 questions**

### Acronyms & Abbreviations

#### ACR-004

**Q:** What does "ADDS" stand for?

- **A.** Aviation Digital Data Services. ✅
- **B.** Automated Drone Detection System.
- **C.** Airport Departure Data Sheet.

**✅ Answer:** A — Aviation Digital Data Services.

**Explanation:** ADDS stands for Aviation Digital Data Services — a web-based weather resource for pilots that includes METARs, TAFs, PIREPs, and other aviation weather products.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** ADDS is not related to drone detection. It is a weather data service.
- **C:** ADDS is not a departure data sheet. It provides digital weather products for aviation.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: aviationweather.gov | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, adds, weather_

---

#### ACR-010

**Q:** What does "AIRMET" stand for?

- **A.** Airman's Meteorological Information. ✅
- **B.** Airport Meteorological Terminal.
- **C.** Airspace Instrument Rating Evaluation Tool.

**✅ Answer:** A — Airman's Meteorological Information.

**Explanation:** AIRMET stands for Airman's Meteorological Information — advisories for significant weather conditions at intensities lower than those requiring a SIGMET, including IFR conditions, mountain obscuration, turbulence, strong surface winds, and icing.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** AIRMET is not an airport terminal. It is a weather advisory product.
- **C:** AIRMET has nothing to do with instrument ratings. It is a weather advisory.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §7-1-5 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, airmet, weather-advisory_

---

#### ACR-014

**Q:** In weather reports, what does "CB" stand for?

- **A.** Cumulonimbus. ✅
- **B.** Cloud Base.
- **C.** Controlled Boundary.

**✅ Answer:** A — Cumulonimbus.

**Explanation:** CB stands for Cumulonimbus — towering thunderstorm clouds associated with severe turbulence, icing, hail, lightning, and wind shear. These are hazardous to all aircraft.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Cloud Base is not abbreviated CB. CB specifically means Cumulonimbus thunderstorm clouds.
- **C:** CB has nothing to do with boundaries. It identifies dangerous thunderstorm clouds.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §7-1-29 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, cb, thunderstorm, weather_

---

#### ACR-037

**Q:** What does "ISA" stand for?

- **A.** International Standard Atmosphere. ✅
- **B.** Instrument Safety Advisory.
- **C.** Interstate Security Area.

**✅ Answer:** A — International Standard Atmosphere.

**Explanation:** ISA stands for International Standard Atmosphere — a model atmosphere used as a reference (15°C at sea level, standard pressure 29.92 inHg). Deviations from ISA affect density altitude.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** ISA is not a safety advisory. It is the standard model atmosphere used in aviation calculations.
- **C:** ISA has nothing to do with security. It is the International Standard Atmosphere.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: ICAO Doc 7488 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, isa, atmosphere, performance_

---

#### ACR-050

**Q:** What does "NOAA" stand for?

- **A.** National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration. ✅
- **B.** National Operations and Aviation Authority.
- **C.** North American Observation Agency.

**✅ Answer:** A — National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration.

**Explanation:** NOAA stands for National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration — the agency that provides weather data including aviation weather products.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** NOAA is not an aviation authority. It focuses on oceanic and atmospheric science.
- **C:** NOAA is not an observation agency. It is the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: noaa.gov | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, noaa, weather_

---

#### ACR-057

**Q:** What does "SIGMET" stand for?

- **A.** Significant Meteorological Information. ✅
- **B.** Signal Meteorological Emergency Transmission.
- **C.** Standard Instrument Ground Meteorological Terminal.

**✅ Answer:** A — Significant Meteorological Information.

**Explanation:** SIGMET stands for Significant Meteorological Information — an advisory for weather significant to the safety of all aircraft, covering severe turbulence, severe icing, and widespread dust or sandstorms.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** SIGMET is not an emergency transmission. It is a weather advisory product.
- **C:** SIGMET has nothing to do with ground terminals. It is Significant Meteorological Information.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §7-1-5 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, sigmet, weather-advisory_

---

#### ACR-059

**Q:** What does "TCU" stand for in weather reports?

- **A.** Towering Cumulus. ✅
- **B.** Terminal Control Unit.
- **C.** Turbulence Caution Update.

**✅ Answer:** A — Towering Cumulus.

**Explanation:** TCU stands for Towering Cumulus — rapidly growing cumulus clouds that may develop into cumulonimbus (thunderstorm) clouds. An indicator of atmospheric instability.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** TCU in weather context is not a control unit. It identifies developing storm clouds.
- **C:** TCU is not a turbulence update. It describes a specific cloud type.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §7-1-29 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, tcu, clouds, weather_

---

#### ACR-070

**Q:** What does "WST" stand for?

- **A.** Convective Significant Meteorological Information (Convective SIGMET). ✅
- **B.** Weather Station Terminal.
- **C.** Wind Shear Test.

**✅ Answer:** A — Convective Significant Meteorological Information (Convective SIGMET).

**Explanation:** WST stands for Convective Significant Meteorological Information — commonly called a Convective SIGMET, issued for severe thunderstorms, tornadoes, and significant convective activity.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** WST is not a weather station. It is a convective weather advisory product.
- **C:** WST does not refer to wind shear testing. It is a Convective SIGMET.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §7-1-5 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, wst, convective-sigmet, weather_

---

#### ACR-073

**Q:** What does "METAR" stand for?

- **A.** Meteorological Aerodrome Report. ✅
- **B.** Main Environmental Terminal Analysis Report.
- **C.** Magnetic Elevation Tracking and Recording.

**✅ Answer:** A — Meteorological Aerodrome Report.

**Explanation:** METAR stands for Meteorological Aerodrome Report — a standardized current weather observation for an airport, usually issued hourly.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** METAR is not an environmental analysis. It is a standardized airport weather observation.
- **C:** METAR has nothing to do with elevation tracking. It reports current weather conditions.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §7-1-30 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, metar, weather-reports_

---

#### ACR-074

**Q:** What does "TAF" stand for?

- **A.** Terminal Aerodrome Forecast. ✅
- **B.** Traffic Advisory Frequency.
- **C.** Terrain Analysis File.

**✅ Answer:** A — Terminal Aerodrome Forecast.

**Explanation:** TAF stands for Terminal Aerodrome Forecast — a forecast of expected weather within 5 statute miles of the airport terminal area, usually covering 24 to 30 hours.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** TAF is not a frequency. It is a weather forecast for the airport area.
- **C:** TAF has nothing to do with terrain files. It forecasts airport weather conditions.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM §7-1-31 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, taf, weather-forecast_

### Cloud Clearance

#### WX-035

**Q:** When operating a small UAS near clouds, what are the minimum distance requirements under 14 CFR Part 107?

- **A.** 500 ft below clouds and 1,000 ft horizontally
- **B.** 500 ft below clouds and 2,000 ft horizontally ✅
- **C.** 1,000 ft below clouds and 2,000 ft horizontally
- **D.** Clear of clouds — no specific distance required

**✅ Answer:** B — 500 ft below clouds and 2,000 ft horizontally

**Explanation:** Under 14 CFR §107.51(c), a small UAS must remain at least 500 feet below the clouds and at least 2,000 feet horizontally from the clouds. This ensures visibility and separation from IFR traffic that may be descending through clouds.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** The horizontal distance is 2,000 ft, not 1,000 ft.
- **C:** The vertical distance is 500 ft below, not 1,000 ft.
- **D:** Part 107 specifies exact cloud clearance distances — 'clear of clouds' is not sufficient.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.51 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: cloud-clearance, 500-ft, 2000-ft, visibility_

---

### Density Altitude

#### WX-002

**Q:** What effect does high density altitude have on the efficiency of a UA propeller?

- **A.** Propeller efficiency is increased.
- **B.** Propeller efficiency is decreased. ✅
- **C.** Density altitude does not affect propeller efficiency.

**✅ Answer:** B — Propeller efficiency is decreased.

**Explanation:** High density altitude means the air is less dense. A propeller is an airfoil that produces thrust by accelerating air — in thinner air, the propeller moves less air mass per revolution, decreasing its efficiency and the aircraft's overall performance.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Thinner air reduces propeller efficiency — it does not increase it.
- **C:** Density altitude significantly affects propeller efficiency because propellers depend on air density to generate thrust.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1a | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 11 | Source: UAG Q4 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: density-altitude, propeller, performance_

---

#### WX-010

**Q:** Which combination of atmospheric conditions will reduce aircraft takeoff and climb performance?

- **A.** Low temperature, low relative humidity, and low density altitude.
- **B.** High temperature, low relative humidity, and low density altitude.
- **C.** High temperature, high relative humidity, and high density altitude. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — High temperature, high relative humidity, and high density altitude.

**Explanation:** High temperature, high humidity, and high density altitude all result in less dense air. Less dense air reduces engine power output, propeller efficiency, and aerodynamic lift — all of which degrade takeoff and climb performance.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Low temperature, low humidity, and low density altitude create dense air, which improves performance.
- **B:** While high temperature hurts performance, low humidity and low density altitude help it — the combination in C is the worst case.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1a | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 11 | Source: SPA Q27 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: density-altitude, performance, temperature, humidity_

---

#### WX-042

**Q:** How does high density altitude affect a small unmanned aircraft?

- **A.** Increases lift and improves battery efficiency.
- **B.** Has no significant effect on performance.
- **C.** Reduces lift, decreases propeller efficiency, and increases battery consumption. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Reduces lift, decreases propeller efficiency, and increases battery consumption.

**Explanation:** High density altitude means the air is less dense (caused by high temperature, high humidity, or high elevation). Less dense air reduces lift because the wings/rotors move less air mass, decreases propeller efficiency for the same reason, and increases battery consumption because motors must work harder to maintain flight.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Thinner air at high density altitude reduces lift and propeller efficiency — it does not increase them.
- **B:** Density altitude has a significant negative effect on all aspects of sUAS performance.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1a | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 11 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: density-altitude, performance, lift, propeller, battery_

---

### Extended Terms

#### EXT-003

**Q:** What is a METAR?

- **A.** Meteorological Aerodrome Report — current airport weather observation, usually issued hourly. ✅
- **B.** Military Emergency Terminal Airspace Report.
- **C.** A 24-hour weather forecast for terminal areas.

**✅ Answer:** A — Meteorological Aerodrome Report — current airport weather observation, usually issued hourly.

**Explanation:** METAR is a Meteorological Aerodrome Report. It is the current airport weather observation, usually issued every hour.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** METAR is a civilian weather reporting format, not a military one.
- **C:** A 24-hour forecast is a TAF, not a METAR.

#### EXT-004

**Q:** What is a TAF?

- **A.** Terminal Aerodrome Forecast — forecast of expected weather within 5 SM of the airport terminal area, usually for 24 or 30 hours. ✅
- **B.** Turbulence Alert Forecast — warnings for severe turbulence.
- **C.** Temperature and Frontal chart issued daily by the NWS.

**✅ Answer:** A — Terminal Aerodrome Forecast — forecast of expected weather within 5 SM of the airport terminal area, usually for 24 or 30 hours.

**Explanation:** TAF stands for Terminal Aerodrome Forecast. It is a forecast of expected weather within 5 statute miles of the airport, usually covering 24 hours (sometimes 30 hours).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Turbulence warnings are issued as AIRMETs or SIGMETs, not TAFs.
- **C:** TAF is a forecast product, not a chart product.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 7-1-28 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: weather, taf, forecast_

---

#### EXT-005

**Q:** What is an AIRMET?

- **A.** Advisory for significant weather affecting aircraft operations at intensities lower than a SIGMET — examples include IFR conditions, mountain obscuration, turbulence, icing. ✅
- **B.** An emergency weather alert for all aircraft regardless of type.
- **C.** A routine hourly weather broadcast at airports with ATIS.

**✅ Answer:** A — Advisory for significant weather affecting aircraft operations at intensities lower than a SIGMET — examples include IFR conditions, mountain obscuration, turbulence, icing.

**Explanation:** An AIRMET (Airmen's Meteorological Information) advises of significant weather at intensities lower than those requiring a SIGMET. Examples include IFR conditions, mountain obscuration, turbulence, strong surface winds, icing, and freezing levels.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** SIGMETs cover more severe weather hazards significant to all aircraft.
- **C:** ATIS broadcasts are local; AIRMETs are area-wide weather advisories.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 7-1-6 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: weather, airmet, advisories_

---

#### EXT-006

**Q:** What is a SIGMET?

- **A.** Weather advisory significant to the safety of all aircraft, covering hazards such as severe turbulence, severe icing, and widespread dust or sandstorms reducing visibility below 3 miles. ✅
- **B.** A signal-based meteorological alert sent via radio.
- **C.** A low-level weather advisory for small drones only.

**✅ Answer:** A — Weather advisory significant to the safety of all aircraft, covering hazards such as severe turbulence, severe icing, and widespread dust or sandstorms reducing visibility below 3 miles.

**Explanation:** A SIGMET (Significant Meteorological Information) is a weather advisory significant to the safety of all aircraft. It covers hazards such as severe turbulence, severe icing, and widespread dust/sandstorms reducing visibility below 3 miles.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** SIGMETs are textual weather products, not radio signals.
- **C:** SIGMETs affect all aircraft, not just drones.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 7-1-6 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: weather, sigmet, advisories_

---

#### EXT-007

**Q:** What are AWOS and ASOS?

- **A.** Automated weather systems at airports that collect and disseminate airport weather information. ✅
- **B.** Air traffic control communication frequencies for weather broadcasts.
- **C.** Military weather satellites.

**✅ Answer:** A — Automated weather systems at airports that collect and disseminate airport weather information.

**Explanation:** AWOS (Automated Weather Observing System) and ASOS (Automated Surface Observing System) are automated weather systems at airports that collect and disseminate airport weather information.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** AWOS/ASOS are weather observation systems, not communication frequencies.
- **C:** Both are ground-based airport systems, not satellites.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 7-1-12 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: weather, awos, asos_

#### EXT-016

**Q:** When using aviation weather reports and charts, what unit is used for visibility versus distances?

- **A.** Statute miles (SM) for weather visibility; nautical miles (NM) for aviation distance and speed references such as knots. ✅
- **B.** Nautical miles for everything in aviation.
- **C.** Kilometers for weather visibility; miles for navigation.

**✅ Answer:** A — Statute miles (SM) for weather visibility; nautical miles (NM) for aviation distance and speed references such as knots.

**Explanation:** Statute miles (SM) are used for weather visibility reporting. Nautical miles (NM) are used for aviation distance and speed references (knots).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Visibility is reported in statute miles, not nautical miles.
- **C:** U.S. aviation uses miles and nautical miles, not kilometers.

### Thunderstorms

#### WX-036

**Q:** What is the second stage of a thunderstorm's life cycle?

- **A.** Cumulus stage — characterized by strong updrafts
- **B.** Mature stage — characterized by both updrafts and downdrafts, heaviest precipitation ✅
- **C.** Dissipating stage — characterized by downdrafts only
- **D.** Anvil stage — characterized by the formation of an anvil cloud top

**✅ Answer:** B — Mature stage — characterized by both updrafts and downdrafts, heaviest precipitation

**Explanation:** A thunderstorm has three stages: (1) Cumulus stage — strong updrafts build the storm; (2) Mature stage — the most dangerous stage, with both updrafts and downdrafts, heavy rain, lightning, hail, and turbulence; (3) Dissipating stage — downdrafts dominate and the storm weakens.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** The cumulus stage is the FIRST stage, not the second.
- **C:** The dissipating stage is the THIRD (final) stage.
- **D:** The anvil top forms during the mature or dissipating stage but is not a named stage of the thunderstorm life cycle.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K3 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: thunderstorm, mature-stage, storm-lifecycle_

---

#### WX-040

**Q:** What are the three stages of a thunderstorm?

- **A.** Towering, dissipating, and forming.
- **B.** Cumulus, mature, and dissipating. ✅
- **C.** Initial, building, and final.

**✅ Answer:** B — Cumulus, mature, and dissipating.

**Explanation:** The three stages of a thunderstorm are: (1) Cumulus stage — characterized by strong updrafts that build the cloud vertically; (2) Mature stage — the most dangerous, with both updrafts and downdrafts, heavy precipitation, lightning, hail, and turbulence; (3) Dissipating stage — dominated by downdrafts as the storm weakens and precipitation diminishes.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** 'Towering, dissipating, and forming' is not the correct terminology. The standard FAA stages are cumulus, mature, and dissipating.
- **C:** 'Initial, building, and final' is not recognized terminology for thunderstorm stages.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K3 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: thunderstorm, cumulus-stage, mature-stage, dissipating-stage_

### Verified Rules

#### REG-V-015

**Q:** What is the minimum flight visibility requirement under Part 107?

- **A.** 3 statute miles as observed from the control station. ✅
- **B.** 1 statute mile at any altitude below 400 ft AGL.
- **C.** 5 statute miles for operations in Class G airspace.

**✅ Answer:** A — 3 statute miles as observed from the control station.

**Explanation:** The minimum flight visibility under Part 107 is 3 statute miles as observed from the control station.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 1 SM is below the required minimum — Part 107 requires at least 3 SM.
- **C:** The standard is 3 SM regardless of airspace class (in uncontrolled Class G).

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.51(c) | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: weather, visibility, minimums_

---

#### REG-V-016

**Q:** What are the minimum cloud clearance requirements under Part 107?

- **A.** 500 ft below clouds and 2,000 ft horizontally from clouds. ✅
- **B.** Clear of clouds at all times.
- **C.** 1,000 ft below and 1,000 ft horizontally from clouds.

**✅ Answer:** A — 500 ft below clouds and 2,000 ft horizontally from clouds.

**Explanation:** Part 107 requires minimum cloud clearance of 500 ft below clouds and 2,000 ft horizontally from clouds.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 'Clear of clouds' is the VFR rule for Class B airspace, not the Part 107 standard.
- **C:** 1,000 ft below and 1,000 ft horizontal are not the Part 107 minimums.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR 107.51(b) | Source: Part 107 Verified Rules | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: weather, cloud-clearance, minimums_

### Weather Reports

#### WX-003

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 12.) What are the current conditions for Chicago Midway Airport (KMDW)?

📊 _Refer to: figure-12_

- **A.** Sky 700 feet overcast, visibility 1-1/2SM, rain. ✅
- **B.** Sky 7,000 feet overcast, visibility 1-1/2SM, heavy rain.
- **C.** Sky 700 feet overcast, visibility 11, occasionally 2SM, with rain.

**✅ Answer:** A — Sky 700 feet overcast, visibility 1-1/2SM, rain.

**Explanation:** Reading the METAR for KMDW: OVC007 means overcast at 700 feet AGL. The visibility is 1-1/2 statute miles with rain (RA). METAR cloud heights are reported in hundreds of feet AGL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** OVC007 means 700 feet, not 7,000 feet. METAR cloud heights are in hundreds of feet.
- **C:** The visibility is 1-1/2SM, not 11SM. The OVC007 is 700 feet overcast.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K2 | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 12; AIM 7-1-31 | Source: UAG Q25 | Difficulty: 3/5 | Tags: metar, weather-reports, aviation-weather_

---

#### WX-004

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 12.) The wind direction and velocity at KJFK is from

📊 _Refer to: figure-12_

- **A.** 180° true at 4 knots. ✅
- **B.** 180° magnetic at 4 knots.
- **C.** 040° true at 18 knots.

**✅ Answer:** A — 180° true at 4 knots.

**Explanation:** METAR wind reports give direction in degrees true north and speed in knots. The KJFK METAR shows 18004KT, meaning wind from 180° true at 4 knots.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** METAR wind directions are referenced to true north, not magnetic north.
- **C:** 040° at 18 knots is a misreading — the format is dddssKT where ddd=direction and ss=speed.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K2 | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 12; AIM 7-1-31 | Source: UAG Q26 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: metar, wind, true-north_

#### WX-008

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 15.) What is the valid period for the TAF for KMEM?

📊 _Refer to: figure-15_

- **A.** 1200Z to 1200Z. ✅
- **B.** 1200Z to 1800Z.
- **C.** 1800Z to 2400Z.

**✅ Answer:** A — 1200Z to 1200Z.

**Explanation:** The TAF (Terminal Aerodrome Forecast) for KMEM covers a 24-hour period from 1200Z to 1200Z, as shown in the forecast header. Standard TAFs are valid for 24 or 30 hours.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 1200Z to 1800Z would only be a 6-hour period, which is not a standard TAF validity period.
- **C:** 1800Z to 2400Z would only be a 6-hour period and does not match the issued TAF validity.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K2 | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 15; AIM 7-1-31 | Source: SPA Q7 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: taf, forecast, weather-reports_

#### WX-009

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 17.) What wind is forecast for STL at 9,000 feet?

📊 _Refer to: figure-17_

- **A.** 230° true at 32 knots. ✅
- **B.** 230° true at 25 knots.
- **C.** 230° magnetic at 25 knots.

**✅ Answer:** A — 230° true at 32 knots.

**Explanation:** The Winds and Temperatures Aloft Forecast shows the wind for STL at 9,000 feet as 2332+02, meaning 230° true at 32 knots (temperature +2°C). Winds aloft forecasts always use true north for direction.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 2325+07 is the STL value at 6,000 feet, not 9,000 feet.
- **C:** Winds aloft forecasts reference true north, not magnetic north.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K2 | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 17; AIM 7-1-27 | Source: SPA Q8 | Difficulty: 3/5 | Tags: winds-aloft, forecast, true-north_
