### Weather Theory

#### WX-001

**Q:** What are characteristics of a moist, unstable air mass?

- **A.** Turbulence and showery precipitation. ✅
- **B.** Poor visibility and smooth air.
- **C.** Haze and smoke.

**✅ Answer:** A — Turbulence and showery precipitation.

**Explanation:** A moist, unstable air mass is characterized by cumuliform clouds, turbulence, and showery precipitation. Unstable air has a tendency to rise, creating convective activity.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Poor visibility and smooth air are characteristics of a stable air mass, not unstable.
- **C:** Haze and smoke are associated with stable air conditions where pollutants are trapped near the surface.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1d | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: UAG Q1 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: unstable-air, precipitation, turbulence_

---

#### WX-005

**Q:** What are the characteristics of stable air?

- **A.** Good visibility and steady precipitation.
- **B.** Poor visibility and steady precipitation. ✅
- **C.** Poor visibility and intermittent precipitation.

**✅ Answer:** B — Poor visibility and steady precipitation.

**Explanation:** Stable air is characterized by stratiform clouds, poor visibility (due to haze, fog, or smog trapped near the surface), smooth air, and steady (continuous) precipitation rather than showery.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Good visibility is typically not associated with stable air — stable conditions trap pollutants and moisture, reducing visibility.
- **C:** Intermittent/showery precipitation is characteristic of unstable air, not stable air.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1c | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: UAG Q38 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: stable-air, visibility, precipitation_

---

#### WX-006

**Q:** You have received an outlook briefing from flight service through 1800wxbrief.com. The briefing indicates you can expect a low-level temperature inversion with high relative humidity. What weather conditions would you expect?

- **A.** Smooth air, poor visibility, fog, haze, or low clouds. ✅
- **B.** Light wind shear, poor visibility, haze, and light rain.
- **C.** Turbulent air, poor visibility, fog, low stratus type clouds, and showery precipitation.

**✅ Answer:** A — Smooth air, poor visibility, fog, haze, or low clouds.

**Explanation:** A temperature inversion traps air near the surface, creating very stable conditions. Combined with high humidity, this produces smooth air, poor visibility, and potential fog, haze, or low clouds. Pollutants and moisture are trapped below the inversion layer.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** While poor visibility is correct, wind shear and light rain are not the primary characteristics of an inversion with high humidity.
- **C:** Turbulence and showery precipitation are characteristic of unstable air, not the stable conditions created by a temperature inversion.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1j | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: UAG Q39 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: temperature-inversion, fog, visibility, stable-air_

---

#### WX-039

**Q:** What type of cloud is associated with stable air and poor visibility?

- **A.** Cumulus.
- **B.** Cumulonimbus.
- **C.** Stratus. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Stratus.

**Explanation:** Stratus clouds form in stable air when a layer of moist air is cooled uniformly. They are flat, layered clouds associated with poor visibility, smooth air, and steady (continuous) precipitation such as drizzle. Stable conditions trap moisture and pollutants near the surface.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Cumulus clouds are associated with unstable air and convective activity, not stable air.
- **B:** Cumulonimbus clouds are thunderstorm clouds associated with very unstable air, turbulence, and heavy precipitation.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1c | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: stable-air, stratus, clouds, visibility_

---

#### WX-041

**Q:** What condition is most likely to cause turbulence?

- **A.** A continuous high-pressure system.
- **B.** Overcast skies with steady precipitation.
- **C.** Uneven heating of the earth's surface. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Uneven heating of the earth's surface.

**Explanation:** Uneven heating of the earth's surface creates convective currents — rising columns of warm air and sinking columns of cooler air. These vertical air movements produce turbulence. Different surface types (water, pavement, vegetation, sand) absorb and radiate heat at different rates, creating localized temperature differences that drive convective turbulence.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** A continuous high-pressure system typically produces stable conditions with smooth air, not turbulence.
- **B:** Overcast skies with steady precipitation are associated with stable air and smooth conditions, not turbulence.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1d | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: turbulence, convection, uneven-heating_

---

#### WX-043

**Q:** What is the primary cause of all weather changes?

- **A.** Variation of solar energy received by the earth. ✅
- **B.** Changes in barometric pressure.
- **C.** Movement of the jet stream.

**✅ Answer:** A — Variation of solar energy received by the earth.

**Explanation:** The sun is the primary source of energy that drives all weather. Unequal heating of the earth's surface (due to latitude, terrain, water vs land, cloud cover, and seasons) creates temperature differences that produce pressure differences, wind, and moisture changes — the fundamental building blocks of all weather phenomena.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Barometric pressure changes are a result of unequal solar heating, not the primary cause of weather changes.
- **C:** The jet stream influences weather patterns but is itself a product of unequal solar heating of the earth.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1a | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: solar-energy, weather-cause, weather-theory_

---

#### WX-044

**Q:** What are the characteristics of unstable air?

- **A.** Poor visibility, steady precipitation, and stratus clouds.
- **B.** Turbulence, good visibility, and showery precipitation. ✅
- **C.** Smooth air, steady precipitation, and fog.

**✅ Answer:** B — Turbulence, good visibility, and showery precipitation.

**Explanation:** Unstable air is characterized by turbulence (convective currents), good visibility (vertical mixing disperses pollutants), showery (intermittent) precipitation, and cumuliform clouds. The rising air currents in unstable conditions create bumpy conditions but also clear the air of haze and smog.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Poor visibility, steady precipitation, and stratus clouds are characteristics of stable air, not unstable air.
- **C:** Smooth air, steady precipitation, and fog are all associated with stable atmospheric conditions.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1d | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: unstable-air, turbulence, visibility, precipitation_

---

#### WX-045

**Q:** What produces the global circulation of winds?

- **A.** The rotation of the earth and differences in air pressure. ✅
- **B.** Tidal forces from the moon.
- **C.** Mountain ranges deflecting airflow.

**✅ Answer:** A — The rotation of the earth and differences in air pressure.

**Explanation:** Global wind circulation is produced by two main factors: (1) unequal heating of the earth's surface creates pressure differences that cause air to flow from high to low pressure, and (2) the rotation of the earth (Coriolis effect) deflects this airflow, creating the prevailing wind patterns (trade winds, westerlies, polar easterlies).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Tidal forces from the moon affect ocean tides but do not produce global wind circulation patterns.
- **C:** Mountain ranges can deflect local airflow and create turbulence, but they do not produce the global circulation of winds.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1b | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: global-winds, coriolis-effect, pressure-difference, earth-rotation_

---

### Wind Shear

#### WX-007

**Q:** A pilot can expect a wind-shear zone in a temperature inversion whenever the windspeed at 2,000 to 4,000 feet above the surface is at least

- **A.** 10 knots.
- **B.** 15 knots.
- **C.** 25 knots. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — 25 knots.

**Explanation:** A significant wind-shear zone can be expected in a temperature inversion when the wind speed at 2,000 to 4,000 feet above the surface is at least 25 knots. The inversion creates a boundary where wind conditions change dramatically over a short vertical distance.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** 10 knots is too low to create a significant wind shear zone in a temperature inversion.
- **B:** 15 knots is not sufficient — 25 knots is the threshold for expecting a wind-shear zone.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K1j | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: SPA Q6 | Difficulty: 3/5 | Tags: wind-shear, temperature-inversion, wind-speed_

---

#### WX-037

**Q:** What is wind shear, and why is it hazardous to small UAS operations?

- **A.** Wind shear is a steady, constant wind that is easy to compensate for
- **B.** Wind shear is a rapid change in wind speed or direction over a short distance, which can cause sudden loss of control ✅
- **C.** Wind shear only occurs above 10,000 ft MSL and is not relevant to UAS operations
- **D.** Wind shear describes the rotation of wind in a hurricane

**✅ Answer:** B — Wind shear is a rapid change in wind speed or direction over a short distance, which can cause sudden loss of control

**Explanation:** Wind shear is a rapid change in wind velocity (speed or direction) over a short distance. It can occur horizontally or vertically and is especially hazardous during takeoff, landing, and low-altitude operations. A sudden wind shear can cause a sUAS to lose lift, gain unexpected altitude, or become uncontrollable.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Wind shear is the opposite — it is a sudden, unpredictable change, not a steady wind.
- **C:** Wind shear is most dangerous at low altitudes, making it directly relevant to sUAS operations.
- **D:** Wind shear is not specific to hurricanes — it occurs in many meteorological conditions.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K2 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: wind-shear, hazardous-weather, low-altitude_

---

#### WX-038

**Q:** At what altitude(s) can wind shear be expected?

- **A.** Only at altitudes above 3,000 feet AGL.
- **B.** Only at altitudes below 2,000 feet AGL.
- **C.** At any altitude. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — At any altitude.

**Explanation:** Wind shear can occur at any altitude. While it is most commonly associated with low-level phenomena like temperature inversions and frontal boundaries, wind shear also occurs at higher altitudes near the jet stream, around thunderstorms, and in mountain wave turbulence.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Wind shear is not restricted to altitudes above 3,000 feet — some of the most hazardous wind shear occurs at low altitudes near the surface.
- **B:** While low-level wind shear is well-known, wind shear can and does occur at all altitudes, including near the jet stream and around convective weather.

_ACS: UA.III.B.K2 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 12 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: wind-shear, altitude, hazardous-weather_

## Operations

**41 questions**

### A Remote Pilot

#### KUP-038

**Q:** A remote pilot is conducting a small unmanned aircraft operation over open farmland. A visual observer (VO) is assisting the remote pilot by tracking the aircraft using binoculars to maintain visual line of sight. Is this operation compliant with Part 107?

- **A.** Yes, a visual observer may use binoculars to maintain visual line of sight
- **B.** Yes, as long as the remote pilot can see the aircraft at all times
- **C.** No, a visual observer must maintain visual line of sight using unaided vision ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — No, a visual observer must maintain visual line of sight using unaided vision

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide.

_Citation: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide | Source: knokegeupdate-docx-supplement | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: knokegeupdate-supplement, operations_

---

### A Visual Observer

#### KUP-041

**Q:** A visual observer (VO) sees a manned aircraft approaching the small unmanned aircraft but fails to notify the remote pilot in command (RPIC). What is the RPIC's responsibility in this situation?

- **A.** The RPIC is still responsible for avoiding the manned aircraft and must take immediate action ✅
- **B.** The VO's failure relieves the RPIC of responsibility
- **C.** The RPIC may continue the operation since the VO saw the aircraft

**✅ Answer:** A — The RPIC is still responsible for avoiding the manned aircraft and must take immediate action

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide.

_Citation: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide | Source: knokegeupdate-docx-supplement | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: knokegeupdate-supplement, operations_

### Acronyms & Abbreviations

#### ACR-049

**Q:** What does "NM" stand for in aviation?

- **A.** Nautical Miles. ✅
- **B.** Normal Mode.
- **C.** Navigation Marker.

**✅ Answer:** A — Nautical Miles.

**Explanation:** NM stands for Nautical Miles — used for aviation distances and speed references (knots). One nautical mile equals approximately 1.15 statute miles.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** NM does not refer to an operating mode. It is a unit of distance.
- **C:** NM is not a navigation marker. It stands for Nautical Miles.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM Glossary | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, nm, navigation_

---

#### ACR-058

**Q:** What does "SOP" stand for?

- **A.** Standard Operating Procedures. ✅
- **B.** Safety Operations Protocol.
- **C.** Scheduled Operating Plan.

**✅ Answer:** A — Standard Operating Procedures.

**Explanation:** SOP stands for Standard Operating Procedures — documented procedures to ensure consistency and safety in operations.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** SOP does not specifically stand for Safety Operations Protocol, although SOPs improve safety.
- **C:** SOP is not a scheduling plan. It is Standard Operating Procedures.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-2 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, sop, procedures_

---

#### ACR-061

**Q:** What does "TN" stand for in navigation?

- **A.** True North. ✅
- **B.** Terminal Navigation.
- **C.** Transition Notice.

**✅ Answer:** A — True North.

**Explanation:** TN stands for True North — the direction toward the geographic North Pole. It differs from magnetic north by the amount of magnetic variation.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** TN is not about terminal navigation. It refers to the geographic North Pole direction.
- **C:** TN does not mean Transition Notice. It stands for True North.

_ACS: UA.III.A.K1 | Citation: AIM Glossary | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, tn, navigation, compass_

---

#### ACR-065

**Q:** What does "UTC" stand for?

- **A.** Coordinated Universal Time. ✅
- **B.** Unmanned Traffic Control.
- **C.** Universal Terminal Communications.

**✅ Answer:** A — Coordinated Universal Time.

**Explanation:** UTC stands for Coordinated Universal Time — the global time standard used in aviation. METARs, TAFs, and NOTAMs use UTC (also called Zulu time).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** UTC is not about unmanned traffic. It is the universal time standard.
- **C:** UTC has nothing to do with terminal communications. It is Coordinated Universal Time.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: AIM Glossary | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, utc, zulu-time_

---

### Aeronautical Decision Making

#### OPS-004

**Q:** Identify the hazardous attitude or characteristic a remote pilot displays while taking risks in order to impress others?

- **A.** Impulsivity.
- **B.** Invulnerability.
- **C.** Macho. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Macho.

**Explanation:** The 'Macho' hazardous attitude involves taking risks to prove oneself or impress others. The antidote is: 'Taking chances is foolish.'

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Impulsivity is the urge to do something immediately without thinking. Its antidote is 'Not so fast. Think first.'
- **B:** Invulnerability is the belief that 'It won't happen to me.' Its antidote is 'It could happen to me.'

_ACS: UA.V.D.K4 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: UAG Q12 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: hazardous-attitudes, macho, adm_

---

#### OPS-006

**Q:** When adapting crew resource management (CRM) concepts to the operation of a small UA, CRM must be integrated into

- **A.** the flight portion only.
- **B.** all phases of the operation. ✅
- **C.** the communications only.

**✅ Answer:** B — all phases of the operation.

**Explanation:** CRM must be integrated into all phases of the UAS operation, including preflight planning, briefings, the actual flight, and post-flight debriefing. Effective CRM is not limited to any single phase.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Limiting CRM to only the flight portion ignores critical planning and briefing phases.
- **C:** CRM encompasses much more than just communications — it includes workload management, decision-making, and situational awareness.

_ACS: UA.V.D.K2 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: UAG Q15 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: crm, crew-resource-management, adm_

---

#### OPS-007

**Q:** You have been hired as a remote pilot by a local TV news station to film breaking news with a small UA. You expressed a safety concern and the station manager has instructed you to "fly first, ask questions later." What type of hazardous attitude does this attitude represent?

- **A.** Machismo.
- **B.** Invulnerability.
- **C.** Impulsivity. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Impulsivity.

**Explanation:** 'Fly first, ask questions later' is an example of Impulsivity — the urge to act immediately without adequate thought. The antidote is 'Not so fast. Think first.'

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Machismo involves taking risks to impress others or prove oneself, not acting without thinking.
- **B:** Invulnerability is the belief that accidents happen to others but not to you.

_ACS: UA.V.D.K4 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: UAG Q16 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: hazardous-attitudes, impulsivity, adm_

---

#### OPS-008

**Q:** A local TV station has hired a remote pilot to operate their small UA to cover news stories. The remote pilot has had multiple near misses with obstacles on the ground and two small UAS accidents. What would be a solution for the news station to improve their operating safety culture?

- **A.** The news station should implement a policy of no more than five crashes/incidents within 6 months.
- **B.** The news station does not need to make any changes; there are times that an accident is unavoidable.
- **C.** The news station should recognize hazardous attitudes and situations and develop standard operating procedures that emphasize safety. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — The news station should recognize hazardous attitudes and situations and develop standard operating procedures that emphasize safety.

**Explanation:** A strong safety culture requires recognizing hazardous attitudes, identifying risks, and developing SOPs that emphasize safety. This is the foundation of Safety Management Systems (SMS).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Setting an acceptable crash limit is the opposite of a safety culture — the goal should be zero incidents.
- **B:** Accepting accidents as unavoidable reflects a poor safety culture and invulnerability hazardous attitude.

_ACS: UA.V.D.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: UAG Q17 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: safety-culture, sms, risk-management_

---

#### OPS-011

**Q:** Safety is an important element for a remote pilot to consider prior to operating an unmanned aircraft system. To prevent the final "link" in the accident chain, a remote pilot must consider which methodology?

- **A.** Crew Resource Management.
- **B.** Safety Management System.
- **C.** Risk Management. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Risk Management.

**Explanation:** Risk Management is the methodology used to identify, assess, and mitigate hazards before they lead to an accident. Breaking the accident chain requires proactive risk assessment at each stage.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** CRM is a component of safe operations but is not the specific methodology for breaking the accident chain.
- **B:** SMS is an organizational framework, while Risk Management is the specific process for preventing individual incidents.

_ACS: UA.V.D.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: UAG Q22 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: risk-management, accident-chain, adm_

---

#### PRAC-006

**Q:** A remote PIC correctly identifies all hazards in the operating environment and understands how these hazards could affect the flight outcome. This best describes

- **A.** risk management.
- **B.** aeronautical decision-making.
- **C.** situational awareness. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — situational awareness.

**Explanation:** Situational awareness is the accurate perception and understanding of all factors affecting the operation, including hazards and how they may change the flight outcome.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Risk management is the process of identifying and mitigating hazards, but the question describes the pilot's accurate understanding of the current operating picture.
- **B:** ADM is the broader decision-making process. Situational awareness is a key input to ADM.

_ACS: UA.V.D.K3 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: remote pic, situational awareness, hazards, adm_

---

#### PRAC-010

**Q:** Which situation best represents an external pressure affecting decision-making during a remote pilot operation?

- **A.** Poor weather conditions.
- **B.** The desire to complete a paid flight on time. ✅
- **C.** Equipment malfunction.

**✅ Answer:** B — The desire to complete a paid flight on time.

**Explanation:** External pressures include schedule demands, client expectations, financial incentives, and self-imposed pressure to complete a mission even when conditions suggest delaying or canceling it.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Poor weather is an operational hazard, not an external pressure.
- **C:** Equipment malfunction is a mechanical issue, not a pressure on the pilot's decision-making.

_ACS: UA.V.D.K3 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: remote pilot, external pressures, adm, risk management_

### After The Preflight

#### KUP-042

**Q:** After the preflight briefing, the remote pilot in command (RPIC) realizes they forgot to inform the crew that the small unmanned aircraft will not use obstacle avoidance systems due to the nature of the operation. What should the RPIC do?

- **A.** Proceed with the operation since the crew is already briefed
- **B.** Stop and update the briefing to ensure the crew understands the limitation ✅
- **C.** Rely on the crew to notice obstacles and adjust accordingly

**✅ Answer:** B — Stop and update the briefing to ensure the crew understands the limitation

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide.

### Airport Operations

#### OPS-001

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 22, area 2.) At Coeur D'Alene which frequency should be used as a Common Traffic Advisory Frequency (CTAF) to monitor airport traffic?

📊 _Refer to: figure-22_

- **A.** 122.05 MHz.
- **B.** 135.075 MHz.
- **C.** 122.8 MHz. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — 122.8 MHz.

**Explanation:** The CTAF for Coeur D'Alene Airport is 122.8 MHz, shown on the sectional chart with the circled 'C' designator. The CTAF is the frequency used to communicate traffic information at non-towered airports.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** 122.05 MHz is not the CTAF for this airport.
- **B:** 135.075 MHz is the AWOS-3 (weather) frequency, not the CTAF.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K6a | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 22 | Source: UAG Q5 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: ctaf, airport-frequency, sectional-chart_

---

#### OPS-002

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 20, area 3.) With ATC authorization, you are operating your small unmanned aircraft approximately 4 SM southeast of Elizabeth City Regional Airport (ECG). What hazard is indicated to be in that area?

📊 _Refer to: figure-20_

- **A.** High density military operations in the vicinity.
- **B.** Unmarked balloon on a cable up to 3,008 feet AGL.
- **C.** Unmarked balloon on a cable up to 3,008 feet MSL. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Unmarked balloon on a cable up to 3,008 feet MSL.

**Explanation:** The sectional chart shows a caution note about an unmarked balloon on a cable up to 3,008 feet MSL in that area. Chart altitudes for obstacles and hazards are given in MSL (mean sea level), not AGL.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** While there may be military operations nearby, the specific hazard shown on the chart is the balloon on a cable.
- **B:** The altitude is listed as 3,008 feet MSL, not AGL. Sectional chart obstacle altitudes are MSL.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K6a | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 20 | Source: UAG Q9 | Difficulty: 3/5 | Tags: hazards, balloon, sectional-chart, msl_

---

#### OPS-003

**Q:** The most comprehensive information on a given airport is provided by

- **A.** the Chart Supplements U.S. (formerly Airport Facility Directory). ✅
- **B.** Notices to Airmen (NOTAMS).
- **C.** Terminal Area Chart (TAC).

**✅ Answer:** A — the Chart Supplements U.S. (formerly Airport Facility Directory).

**Explanation:** The Chart Supplements U.S. (formerly Airport/Facility Directory) provides the most comprehensive data for a given airport, including runway data, services, frequencies, lighting, remarks, and operational notes.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** NOTAMs provide time-critical temporary information, not comprehensive permanent airport data.
- **C:** Terminal Area Charts show a detailed view of the airspace around busy airports but do not contain comprehensive airport-specific data.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K6b | Citation: AIM 9-1-4 (Chart Supplements U.S.) | Source: UAG Q11 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: chart-supplements, airport-directory, airport-info_

---

#### OPS-005

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 26, area 4.) You have been hired to inspect the tower under construction at 46.9N and 98.6W, near Jamestown Regional (JMS). What must you receive prior to flying your unmanned aircraft in this area?

📊 _Refer to: figure-26_

- **A.** Authorization from the military.
- **B.** Authorization from ATC. ✅
- **C.** Authorization from the National Park Service.

**✅ Answer:** B — Authorization from ATC.

**Explanation:** Jamestown Regional Airport has Class E airspace designated for it. Under 14 CFR 107.41, you must receive prior ATC authorization to operate within the surface area of Class E airspace designated for an airport.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** The area near Jamestown Regional is civilian-controlled airspace, not military.
- **C:** The National Park Service does not control airspace — ATC authorization is required.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K6a | Citation: 14 CFR §107.41 | Source: UAG Q13 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: atc-authorization, class-e, airport-vicinity_

---

#### OPS-013

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 21.) What airport is located approximately 47 (degrees) 40 (minutes) N latitude and 101 (degrees) 26 (minutes) W longitude?

📊 _Refer to: figure-21_

- **A.** Mercer County Regional Airport.
- **B.** Semshenko Airport.
- **C.** Garrison Airport. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Garrison Airport.

**Explanation:** Using latitude and longitude coordinates on the sectional chart, the airport at approximately 47°40'N and 101°26'W is Garrison Airport.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Mercer County Regional Airport is located at different coordinates.
- **B:** Semshenko Airport is located at different coordinates on the chart.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K6a | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 21 | Source: UAG Q24 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: latitude-longitude, sectional-chart, airport-identification_

---

#### OPS-014

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 26.) What does the line of latitude at area 4 measure?

📊 _Refer to: figure-26_

- **A.** The degrees of latitude east and west of the Prime Meridian.
- **B.** The degrees of latitude north and south of the equator. ✅
- **C.** The degrees of latitude east and west of the line that passes through Greenwich, England.

**✅ Answer:** B — The degrees of latitude north and south of the equator.

**Explanation:** Lines of latitude run east-west and measure the angular distance north or south of the equator. They range from 0° at the equator to 90° at the poles.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Measurements east and west of the Prime Meridian describe longitude, not latitude.
- **C:** Greenwich, England is the reference for the Prime Meridian (longitude), not for latitude.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K6a | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 16 | Source: UAG Q31 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: latitude, navigation, coordinates_

### Crew Resource Management

#### PRAC-014

**Q:** During a complex drone operation, a visual observer notices another aircraft approaching the operation area but hesitates to inform the remote pilot because they assume the PIC already sees it. This situation best illustrates a breakdown in

- **A.** situational awareness.
- **B.** crew resource management. ✅
- **C.** aeronautical decision-making.

**✅ Answer:** B — crew resource management.

**Explanation:** Crew resource management depends on timely, assertive communication among all participants. Hesitating to speak up about an approaching aircraft is a CRM breakdown.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Situational awareness may degrade as a result, but the root failure described is the crew's poor coordination and communication.
- **C:** ADM is the broader decision process; the scenario specifically describes a crew communication problem.

_ACS: UA.V.D.K2 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: drone, crew resource management, visual observer, communication_

### During A Part

#### KUP-010

**Q:** During a Part 107 operation, may a visual observer (VO) assume control of the small unmanned aircraft during flight?

- **A.** Yes, at any time, if the remote pilot in command authorizes it
- **B.** Yes, but only in an emergency situation
- **C.** No, a visual observer may not manipulate the flight controls ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — No, a visual observer may not manipulate the flight controls

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide.

### Emergency

#### P107-032

**Q:** During an in-flight emergency requiring immediate action, the RPIC may:

- **A.** Deviate from any rule of Part 107 to the extent necessary to meet that emergency ✅
- **B.** Deviate only from altitude rules
- **C.** Never deviate from Part 107 rules

**✅ Answer:** A — Deviate from any rule of Part 107 to the extent necessary to meet that emergency

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from 14 CFR 107.21(a).

_Citation: 14 CFR 107.21(a) | Source: part107-question-bank | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part107-bank, emergency_

---

#### P107-033

**Q:** If the RPIC deviates from Part 107 rules due to an in-flight emergency, a written report must be sent:

- **A.** Immediately after landing
- **B.** Only upon request of the Administrator ✅
- **C.** Within 10 days no matter what

**✅ Answer:** B — Only upon request of the Administrator

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from 14 CFR 107.21(b).

_Citation: 14 CFR 107.21(b) | Source: part107-question-bank | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part107-bank, emergency_

---

#### P107-091

**Q:** If a Part 107 operation requires deviation for emergency, the deviation must be:

- **A.** Only for 30 seconds
- **B.** Only to the extent necessary to meet the emergency ✅
- **C.** Never allowed

**✅ Answer:** B — Only to the extent necessary to meet the emergency

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from 14 CFR 107.21(a).

### Emergency Procedures

#### OPS-010

**Q:** To avoid a possible collision with a manned airplane, you estimate that your small UA climbed to an altitude greater than 600 feet AGL. To whom must you report the deviation?

- **A.** Air Traffic Control.
- **B.** The National Transportation Safety Board.
- **C.** Upon request of the Federal Aviation Administration. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Upon request of the Federal Aviation Administration.

**Explanation:** Under 14 CFR 107.21, in an in-flight emergency requiring immediate action, the remote PIC may deviate from any rule of Part 107 to the extent required to meet that emergency. They must report the deviation to the FAA upon request.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Reporting to ATC is not the specific requirement. The deviation must be reported to the FAA upon their request.
- **B:** The NTSB handles accident reporting, not emergency deviations from operating rules.

_ACS: UA.V.C.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.21 | Source: UAG Q19 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: emergency, deviation, reporting_

---

#### OPS-018

**Q:** When using a small UA in a commercial operation, who is responsible for briefing the participants about emergency procedures?

- **A.** The FAA inspector-in-charge.
- **B.** The lead visual observer.
- **C.** The remote PIC. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — The remote PIC.

**Explanation:** The remote PIC is responsible for all aspects of the operation, including ensuring that all participants and crew members are briefed on emergency procedures before the operation begins.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** FAA inspectors do not brief participants — this is the PIC's responsibility.
- **B:** Visual observers support the PIC but are not responsible for conducting emergency briefings.

_ACS: UA.V.C.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.49 | Source: UAG Q37 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: emergency-briefing, remote-pic, responsibility_

---

#### OPS-100

**Q:** What does a 'lost link' situation mean during a small UAS flight, and what is the required response?

- **A.** The drone's video feed has been lost; the pilot may continue flight using GPS
- **B.** Control link between the ground control station and aircraft is lost; the drone should follow its programmed lost-link procedure and land as soon as practicable ✅
- **C.** The ATC radio link is lost; the pilot must immediately land at the nearest airport
- **D.** Lost link only applies to military drones — Part 107 aircraft are not required to have a lost-link procedure

**✅ Answer:** B — Control link between the ground control station and aircraft is lost; the drone should follow its programmed lost-link procedure and land as soon as practicable

**Explanation:** A 'lost link' (also called lost communications) occurs when the control link between the Remote Pilot In Command and the small UAS is interrupted. The aircraft typically follows a pre-programmed lost-link procedure (hover, return-to-home, or land). The RPIC is responsible for establishing this procedure before flight to ensure the sUAS does not pose a hazard to other aircraft or people on the ground.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Lost link refers to the control link, not just the video/FPV feed.
- **C:** Small UAS are generally not in contact with ATC; the lost-link procedure governs the aircraft's autonomous response.
- **D:** Part 107 pilots are responsible for their aircraft's behavior at all times, including during lost-link events.

_ACS: UA.V.A.K3 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.19 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: lost-link, lost-comm, emergency-procedure, return-to-home_

---

#### PRAC-018

**Q:** During a Part 107 operation, the remote PIC deviates from a regulation to avoid an unexpected manned helicopter that enters the operating area. What must the remote PIC do after the flight?

- **A.** Nothing, because emergency authority permanently resolves the situation.
- **B.** Submit a written report to the FAA only if requested. ✅
- **C.** Immediately notify air traffic control after landing.

**✅ Answer:** B — Submit a written report to the FAA only if requested.

**Explanation:** In an in-flight emergency, the remote PIC may deviate from Part 107 rules to the extent needed to respond safely. The FAA may then require a written report of that deviation on request.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Emergency authority does not eliminate possible follow-up reporting if the FAA asks for it.
- **C:** Immediate ATC notification after landing is not the rule-based reporting requirement described in Part 107.

_ACS: UA.V.C.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.21 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: part 107, emergency procedures, remote pic, deviation_

### In Small Unmanned

#### KUP-011

**Q:** In small unmanned aircraft operations, what best describes the control station?

- **A.** The physical location where the remote pilot stands while monitoring the flight
- **B.** The ground-based control equipment used by the remote pilot to operate the small unmanned aircraft at the pilot's location ✅
- **C.** The launch and recovery area designated for the sUAS operation

**✅ Answer:** B — The ground-based control equipment used by the remote pilot to operate the small unmanned aircraft at the pilot's location

**Explanation:** Answer sourced from FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide.

### Maintenance

#### OPS-015

**Q:** Under what condition should the operator of a small UA establish scheduled maintenance protocol?

- **A.** When the manufacturer does not provide a maintenance schedule. ✅
- **B.** UAS does not need a required maintenance schedule.
- **C.** When the FAA requires you to, following an accident.

**✅ Answer:** A — When the manufacturer does not provide a maintenance schedule.

**Explanation:** When the manufacturer does not provide a maintenance schedule, the operator should establish one. Best practice is to always have a maintenance protocol to ensure the sUAS remains in a condition for safe operation.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Proper maintenance is essential for safe sUAS operations and should always be scheduled.
- **C:** Waiting for the FAA to require maintenance after an accident is reactive and unsafe.

_ACS: UA.V.F.K1 | Citation: AC 107-2A, Section 7.5 (Maintenance and Inspection) | Source: UAG Q32 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: maintenance, inspection, safety_

---

#### OPS-016

**Q:** According to 14 CFR part 107, the responsibility to inspect the small UAS to ensure it is in a safe operating condition rests with the

- **A.** remote pilot-in-command. ✅
- **B.** visual observer.
- **C.** owner of the small UAS.

**✅ Answer:** A — remote pilot-in-command.

**Explanation:** Under 14 CFR 107.15 and 107.49, the remote PIC is responsible for ensuring the sUAS is in a condition for safe operation before each flight.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The visual observer assists the PIC but does not bear the responsibility for aircraft condition.
- **C:** While the owner may maintain the aircraft, the PIC is responsible for verifying safe condition before flight.

_ACS: UA.V.F.K2 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.15, §107.49 | Source: UAG Q33 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: inspection, remote-pic, maintenance_

---

#### PRAC-008

**Q:** What precautions should a remote PIC take to reduce the risk of flight emergencies when using lithium-based batteries?

- **A.** Store the batteries fully discharged.
- **B.** Use batteries that show signs of swelling.
- **C.** Follow the manufacturer's charging and temperature guidelines. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Follow the manufacturer's charging and temperature guidelines.

**Explanation:** Lithium-based batteries should be charged, stored, and used according to the manufacturer's temperature and charging guidance. That reduces the risk of battery failure, fire, or in-flight power loss.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Fully discharging lithium batteries can damage them and may increase failure risk depending on the battery type.
- **B:** Swelling is a warning sign of battery damage and should be treated as an unsafe condition.

_ACS: UA.V.F.K1 | Citation: AC 107-2A, Section 7.5; FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 7 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: remote pic, battery, preflight, maintenance_

### Physiology

#### OPS-012

**Q:** You are a remote pilot for a co-op energy service provider. You are to use your UA to inspect power lines in a remote area 15 hours away from your home office. After the drive, fatigue impacts your abilities to complete your assignment on time. Fatigue can be recognized

- **A.** easily by an experienced pilot.
- **B.** as being in an impaired state. ✅
- **C.** by an ability to overcome sleep deprivation.

**✅ Answer:** B — as being in an impaired state.

**Explanation:** Fatigue is recognized as being in an impaired state. It degrades attention, judgment, and reaction time. A fatigued pilot is an impaired pilot and should not operate an aircraft.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Fatigue is insidious and difficult to self-assess, even for experienced pilots.
- **C:** Sleep deprivation cannot be overcome by willpower alone — only adequate rest addresses fatigue.

_ACS: UA.V.E.K5 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: UAG Q23 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: fatigue, fitness-for-duty, physiology_

---

#### OPS-017

**Q:** Which is true regarding the presence of alcohol within the human body?

- **A.** A small amount of alcohol increases vision acuity.
- **B.** Consuming an equal amount of water will increase the destruction of alcohol and alleviate a hangover.
- **C.** Judgment and decision-making abilities can be adversely affected by even small amounts of alcohol. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Judgment and decision-making abilities can be adversely affected by even small amounts of alcohol.

**Explanation:** Even small amounts of alcohol impair judgment, decision-making, and reaction time. Under 14 CFR 107.27, no person may operate or act as remote PIC while under the influence of alcohol.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Alcohol impairs vision — it does not improve visual acuity.
- **B:** Water does not accelerate the metabolism of alcohol. Only time allows the body to process alcohol.

_ACS: UA.V.E.K2 | Citation: 14 CFR §107.27 | Source: UAG Q36 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: alcohol, impairment, physiology_

---

#### OPS-019

**Q:** When preparing for a night flight, what should an sUAS pilot be aware of after assembling and conducting a preflight of an aircraft while using a bright flashlight or work light?

- **A.** Once adapted to darkness, a person's eyes are relatively immune to bright lights.
- **B.** It takes approximately 30 minutes for a person's eyes to fully adapt to darkness. ✅
- **C.** The person should use a flashlight equipped with LED lights to facilitate their night vision.

**✅ Answer:** B — It takes approximately 30 minutes for a person's eyes to fully adapt to darkness.

**Explanation:** Dark adaptation of the eyes takes approximately 30 minutes. Using bright white lights during preflight will reset this process, so pilots should allow adequate time for their eyes to readjust before operating at night.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Once adapted, the eyes are very sensitive to bright lights — a brief exposure can undo 30 minutes of adaptation.
- **C:** Bright LED lights can actually harm night vision. Red light is preferred for maintaining dark adaptation.

_ACS: UA.V.E.K8 | Citation: AIM 8-1-6 (Night Operations and Visual Illusions) | Source: UAG Q43 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: night-vision, dark-adaptation, physiology_

---

#### PRAC-016

**Q:** As a remote pilot, long extended flights during hot summer temperatures increase the susceptibility to dehydration because

- **A.** hot days increase the rate of water loss in the body. ✅
- **B.** body water loss slows as long as the pilot remains seated at the controls.
- **C.** the body's need for water decreases in hot weather.

**✅ Answer:** A — hot days increase the rate of water loss in the body.

**Explanation:** High temperatures increase perspiration and fluid loss. Without active hydration, a remote pilot is more susceptible to dehydration, fatigue, and degraded judgment.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Remaining seated does not prevent heat-related fluid loss.
- **C:** Hot weather increases, not decreases, the body's need for water.

_ACS: UA.V.E.K5 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: remote pilot, physiology, dehydration, summer operations_

### Radio Communications

#### OPS-009

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 26, area 2.) While monitoring the Cooperstown CTAF you hear an aircraft announce that they are midfield left downwind to RWY 13. Where would the aircraft be relative to the runway?

📊 _Refer to: figure-26_

- **A.** The aircraft is East.
- **B.** The aircraft is South.
- **C.** The aircraft is West. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — The aircraft is West.

**Explanation:** Runway 13 points to a heading of 130°. Left downwind for RWY 13 means the aircraft is flying parallel to the runway but in the opposite direction (heading 310°), on the left side when viewed from the landing direction. This places the aircraft to the west of the runway.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** East would be on the right side of the runway, not the left downwind side.
- **B:** South of the runway would not be the left downwind position for RWY 13.

_ACS: UA.V.A.K3 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 (CTAF Position Reports in the Traffic Pattern) | Source: UAG Q18 | Difficulty: 3/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, radio-communications, downwind_

### Visual Perception

#### PRAC-005

**Q:** While flying a small unmanned aircraft forward, nearby trees appear to move quickly while distant mountains appear to move slowly. This is an example of

- **A.** motion parallax. ✅
- **B.** autokinesis.
- **C.** the Coriolis illusion.

**✅ Answer:** A — motion parallax.

**Explanation:** Motion parallax is the apparent difference in rate of movement of objects at different distances. Nearby objects seem to move faster than distant objects as the aircraft moves forward.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Autokinesis is the illusion that a stationary light is moving when stared at in darkness.
- **C:** The Coriolis illusion is a vestibular illusion caused by head movement during acceleration, not by relative motion of near and far objects.

_ACS: UA.V.E.K6 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 17 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: unmanned aircraft, visual perception, motion parallax, human factors_

- **B.** The remote pilot in command may deviate from Part 107 rules to avoid collision with a manned aircraft. ✅

### Extended Terms

#### EXT-015

**Q:** What is TPA and what is the standard value?

- **A.** Traffic Pattern Altitude — standard is usually 1,000 ft AGL for most propeller aircraft unless otherwise published. ✅
- **B.** Terminal Procedures Altitude — the minimum approach altitude.
- **C.** Tower Pattern Approach — the altitude at which the tower clears aircraft to land.

**✅ Answer:** A — Traffic Pattern Altitude — standard is usually 1,000 ft AGL for most propeller aircraft unless otherwise published.

**Explanation:** TPA stands for Traffic Pattern Altitude. Standard TPA for most propeller aircraft is 1,000 ft AGL unless otherwise published. Drone pilots should stay alert for aircraft using the airport pattern.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Minimum approach altitudes are called MDA or DA, not TPA.
- **C:** TPA is a published pattern altitude, not a clearance altitude.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: airport-ops, tpa, traffic-pattern_

### Figure-Specific Runways

#### APT-017

**Q:** Referring to the Elizabeth City (ECG) sectional excerpt, what is the magnetic heading for Runway 10?

📊 _Refer to: figure-20_

- **A.** 100 degrees. The opposite end (Runway 28) is 280 degrees. ✅
- **B.** 010 degrees. The opposite end (Runway 19) is 190 degrees.
- **C.** 280 degrees. The opposite end (Runway 10) is 100 degrees.

**✅ Answer:** A — 100 degrees. The opposite end (Runway 28) is 280 degrees.

**Explanation:** Runway 10 times 10 equals 100 degrees magnetic. The opposite end is 10 plus 18 equals 28, so Runway 28 at 280 degrees.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 010/190 degrees is Runway 1/19, a different runway.
- **C:** 280 degrees is the heading for Runway 28 (the opposite end).

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 2-3-3; FAA Testing Supplement Figure 20 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: runway, figure-20, ecg, elizabeth-city_

### Runway Markings

#### APT-001

**Q:** What does a runway number physically represent?

- **A.** The runway magnetic compass heading rounded to the nearest 10 degrees, with the last zero dropped. ✅
- **B.** The runway length in hundreds of feet.
- **C.** The runway assigned sequence number at the airport.

**✅ Answer:** A — The runway magnetic compass heading rounded to the nearest 10 degrees, with the last zero dropped.

**Explanation:** A runway number is its magnetic heading divided by 10. Runway 27 = approximately 270 degrees magnetic.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Runway numbers have nothing to do with length.
- **C:** Runway numbers are not arbitrary sequence numbers.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 2-3-3 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Ch. 10 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: runway, markings, headings_

#### APT-002

**Q:** If you are taking off on Runway 9, what magnetic direction are you heading?

- **A.** East (090 degrees magnetic). ✅
- **B.** West (270 degrees magnetic).
- **C.** North (360 degrees magnetic).

**✅ Answer:** A — East (090 degrees magnetic).

**Explanation:** Runway 9 times 10 equals 090 degrees, which is due East.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 270 degrees is Runway 27, the opposite end.
- **C:** 360 degrees is Runway 36.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 2-3-3 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Ch. 10 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: runway, headings, directions_

#### APT-003

**Q:** What is the math rule to find the opposite end of any runway?

- **A.** Add or subtract 18 (representing a 180-degree turnaround). ✅
- **B.** Multiply the runway number by 2.
- **C.** Add 9 to the runway number.

**✅ Answer:** A — Add or subtract 18 (representing a 180-degree turnaround).

**Explanation:** Adding or subtracting 18 gives the opposite runway. Runway 13 plus 18 equals Runway 31.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Multiplying by 2 does not work for this calculation.
- **C:** Adding 9 gives a 90-degree difference, not 180 degrees.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 2-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Math | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: runway, math, opposite-end_

- **A.** Runway 31. ✅
- **B.** Runway 22.
- **C.** Runway 4.

**✅ Answer:** A — Runway 31.

**Explanation:** 13 plus 18 equals 31. The opposite end of Runway 13 is Runway 31 (130 degrees plus 180 degrees equals 310 degrees).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Runway 22 is the opposite of Runway 4, not Runway 13.
- **C:** Runway 4 is 040 degrees magnetic. The opposite of 13 is 31.

- **C.** Grid north.

**✅ Answer:** A — Magnetic north.

**Explanation:** Runway numbers are based on magnetic north because pilots navigate using magnetic compasses.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** True north is used on some maps but not for runway numbering.
- **C:** Grid north is used in military operations.

### Traffic Patterns

#### APT-009

**Q:** What is the standard turn direction for an airport traffic pattern?

- **A.** Left turns, unless marked RP on the sectional chart for Right Pattern. ✅
- **B.** Right turns at all airports.
- **C.** Alternating left and right turns.

**✅ Answer:** A — Left turns, unless marked RP on the sectional chart for Right Pattern.

**Explanation:** The standard traffic pattern uses left turns. Right traffic is used only when specifically designated by RP on the sectional chart or Airport Facility Directory.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Right turns are non-standard and only used when specifically designated.
- **C:** Traffic patterns use consistent turn directions, not alternating.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: 14 CFR 91.126(b) | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, turns, left-pattern_

---

#### APT-010

**Q:** What does the Upwind leg of a traffic pattern mean?

- **A.** The flight path parallel to the landing runway, flying in the same direction as landing. ✅
- **B.** The turn from base to final approach.
- **C.** The flight path perpendicular to the runway.

**✅ Answer:** A — The flight path parallel to the landing runway, flying in the same direction as landing.

**Explanation:** The upwind leg is parallel to the runway in the direction of takeoff and landing. An aircraft flies it just after takeoff before turning crosswind.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The turn from base to final is not the upwind leg.
- **C:** A perpendicular path would be the crosswind or base leg.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, upwind, legs_

---

#### APT-011

**Q:** What does the Downwind leg of a traffic pattern mean?

- **A.** The flight path parallel to the landing runway, flying in the opposite direction of landing. ✅
- **B.** The final straight-in approach to the runway.
- **C.** The climb-out segment after takeoff.

**✅ Answer:** A — The flight path parallel to the landing runway, flying in the opposite direction of landing.

**Explanation:** The downwind leg is flown parallel to the runway but in the opposite direction of the landing heading. For Runway 27 (heading 270 degrees), the downwind heading is 090 degrees.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The final approach is the last segment aligned with the runway, not the downwind.
- **C:** The climb-out after takeoff is the upwind or departure leg.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, downwind, legs_

---

#### APT-012

**Q:** If an aircraft is on the Left Downwind for Runway 27, what compass heading is the aircraft currently flying?

- **A.** 090 degrees (East). ✅
- **B.** 270 degrees (West).
- **C.** 180 degrees (South).

**✅ Answer:** A — 090 degrees (East).

**Explanation:** Downwind is the opposite of the landing heading. Runway 27 equals 270 degrees. The opposite of 270 degrees is 090 degrees (East).

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 270 degrees is the landing heading for Runway 27, not the downwind heading.
- **C:** 180 degrees would be a perpendicular heading, not the downwind for Runway 27.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, downwind, heading-math_

---

#### APT-013

**Q:** If an aircraft is on the Left Downwind for Runway 18, where is the aircraft located in relation to the runway?

- **A.** East of the runway, flying North. ✅
- **B.** West of the runway, flying South.
- **C.** North of the runway, flying East.

**✅ Answer:** A — East of the runway, flying North.

**Explanation:** Left downwind for Runway 18 (heading South 180 degrees): left turns put the pattern to the east. The aircraft flies North (opposite of 180 degrees) on the east side of the runway.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** West is where a right pattern would be. Standard left turns put it east.
- **C:** North of the runway with an east heading would be the base leg.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 3/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, downwind, spatial-reasoning_

---

#### APT-014

**Q:** What is the Base leg of a traffic pattern?

- **A.** The short perpendicular flight path where the aircraft transitions from downwind to final approach. ✅
- **B.** The initial climb-out leg after takeoff.
- **C.** The leg parallel to the runway flown in the landing direction.

**✅ Answer:** A — The short perpendicular flight path where the aircraft transitions from downwind to final approach.

**Explanation:** The base leg is a short perpendicular segment connecting the downwind leg to the final approach. The aircraft descends and turns to align with the runway.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The climb-out after takeoff is the upwind or departure leg.
- **C:** A leg parallel to the runway in the landing direction is the upwind leg.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, base, legs_

---

#### APT-015

**Q:** What is the Final Approach leg?

- **A.** The last straight segment where the aircraft is lined up with the runway centerline to land. ✅
- **B.** The segment where the aircraft circles the airport before landing.
- **C.** The climbout after a touch-and-go landing.

**✅ Answer:** A — The last straight segment where the aircraft is lined up with the runway centerline to land.

**Explanation:** The final approach is the last straight-line segment aligned with the runway centerline, during which the aircraft descends to land.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Circling the airport is part of the pattern, not the final approach specifically.
- **C:** A climbout after a touch-and-go is the upwind or departure leg.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, final-approach, legs_

---

#### APT-016

**Q:** If an aircraft announces it is on Final for Runway 22, what heading is it flying?

- **A.** 220 degrees magnetic. ✅
- **B.** 040 degrees magnetic.
- **C.** 180 degrees magnetic.

**✅ Answer:** A — 220 degrees magnetic.

**Explanation:** On final the aircraft is aligned with the runway heading. Runway 22 equals 220 degrees magnetic.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 040 degrees is Runway 4, the opposite end of Runway 22.
- **C:** 180 degrees is Runway 18, not Runway 22.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, final-approach, heading_

---

#### APT-033

**Q:** What is the standard traffic pattern altitude (TPA) for most propeller-driven aircraft?

- **A.** 1,000 ft AGL unless otherwise published. ✅
- **B.** 500 ft AGL.
- **C.** 1,500 ft AGL.

**✅ Answer:** A — 1,000 ft AGL unless otherwise published.

**Explanation:** The standard TPA is 1,000 ft AGL for propeller aircraft unless otherwise published for the specific airport. Drone pilots should stay aware of aircraft at this altitude.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** 500 ft AGL is below the standard pattern altitude.
- **C:** 1,500 ft AGL is above the standard pattern altitude for propeller aircraft.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: AIM 4-3-3 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: traffic-pattern, altitude, tpa_

### Wind and Landing

#### APT-006

**Q:** In relation to the wind, which direction do manned aircraft normally take off and land?

- **A.** Into the wind. A headwind gives the best lift and shortest runway distance. ✅
- **B.** With the wind. A tailwind increases ground speed.
- **C.** Perpendicular to the wind. A crosswind provides stability.

**✅ Answer:** A — Into the wind. A headwind gives the best lift and shortest runway distance.

**Explanation:** Aircraft take off and land into the wind. A headwind provides additional airflow over the wings, reducing the ground distance needed.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** A tailwind increases ground speed but reduces lift effectiveness, requiring more runway.
- **C:** A crosswind creates lateral drift and is not preferred for takeoff or landing.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 9 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: runway, wind, takeoff, landing_

---

#### APT-007

**Q:** If the wind is blowing heavily from the South (180 degrees), which runway is an aircraft most likely to use: Runway 18 or Runway 36?

- **A.** Runway 18 — flying into the 180-degree wind. ✅
- **B.** Runway 36 — flying away from the wind.
- **C.** Either runway, because wind direction does not matter.

**✅ Answer:** A — Runway 18 — flying into the 180-degree wind.

**Explanation:** Runway 18 means flying a heading of 180 degrees (South). Since the wind is from the South, the aircraft flies into the headwind for optimal performance.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Runway 36 heads North (360 degrees). With a south wind that would be a tailwind, which is undesirable.
- **C:** Wind direction is critical for determining the active runway.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 9 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: runway, wind, active-runway_

---

#### APT-008

**Q:** If the wind is blowing from the West (270 degrees), which runway is active: Runway 9 or Runway 27?

- **A.** Runway 27 — flying into the 270-degree wind. ✅
- **B.** Runway 9 — flying east away from the wind.
- **C.** Both runways are equally usable.

**✅ Answer:** A — Runway 27 — flying into the 270-degree wind.

**Explanation:** Runway 27 means heading 270 degrees (West). With the wind from the West the aircraft flies directly into the headwind.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Runway 9 heads East (090 degrees). With wind from the West that would be a tailwind.
- **C:** Pilots choose the runway that provides the most headwind component.

_ACS: UA.II.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 9 | Source: Part 107 Study Pack Runway Cards | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: runway, wind, active-runway_

## Loading & Performance

**15 questions**

### Acronyms & Abbreviations

#### ACR-011

**Q:** What does "AOA" stand for in aerodynamics?

- **A.** Angle of Attack. ✅
- **B.** Area of Approach.
- **C.** Altitude Over Airport.

**✅ Answer:** A — Angle of Attack.

**Explanation:** AOA stands for Angle of Attack — the angle between the wing's chord line and the relative wind. Exceeding the critical AOA causes a stall.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Area of Approach is not what AOA stands for. It describes the angle of the wing relative to airflow.
- **C:** AOA is not an altitude measurement. It is a fundamental aerodynamic concept.

_ACS: UA.V.A.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 5 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, aoa, aerodynamics_

---

#### ACR-017

**Q:** What does "CG" stand for in aircraft operations?

- **A.** Center of Gravity. ✅
- **B.** Control Group.
- **C.** Course Guidance.

**✅ Answer:** A — Center of Gravity.

**Explanation:** CG stands for Center of Gravity — the balance point of the aircraft system. An improper CG can degrade stability and control.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Control Group is not what CG means in aviation. CG refers to the balance point of the aircraft.
- **C:** CG is not navigation guidance. It is the Center of Gravity.

_ACS: UA.V.A.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 10 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, cg, weight-balance_

---

#### ACR-018

**Q:** What does "CP" stand for in aerodynamics?

- **A.** Center of Pressure. ✅
- **B.** Critical Point.
- **C.** Constant Power.

**✅ Answer:** A — Center of Pressure.

**Explanation:** CP stands for Center of Pressure — the point along the wing chord where the total aerodynamic force is considered to act. Its position changes with angle of attack.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Critical Point is not the aviation meaning of CP. CP describes where aerodynamic forces concentrate.
- **C:** Constant Power is not what CP means. It refers to the Center of Pressure on an airfoil.

_ACS: UA.V.A.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 5 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: acronym, cp, aerodynamics_

---

#### ACR-069

**Q:** What does "W&B" stand for?

- **A.** Weight and Balance. ✅
- **B.** Wind and Barometer.
- **C.** Waypoint and Bearing.

**✅ Answer:** A — Weight and Balance.

**Explanation:** W&B stands for Weight and Balance — the process of verifying that an aircraft's weight is within limits and its center of gravity is within the allowable range.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** W&B does not refer to weather instruments. It is about aircraft weight and its balance point.
- **C:** W&B is not about navigation waypoints. It concerns Weight and Balance.

_ACS: UA.V.A.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 10 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, w-b, weight-balance_

### Aerodynamics

#### LP-003

**Q:** A stall occurs when the smooth airflow over the unmanned airplane's wing is disrupted and the lift degenerates rapidly. This is caused when the wing

- **A.** exceeds the maximum speed.
- **B.** exceeds maximum allowable operating weight.
- **C.** exceeds its critical angle of attack. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — exceeds its critical angle of attack.

**Explanation:** A stall occurs when the wing exceeds its critical angle of attack. At this point, airflow separates from the upper surface of the wing and lift is drastically reduced. A stall can occur at any airspeed and any attitude.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Exceeding maximum speed causes structural concerns, not a stall. Stalls are related to angle of attack.
- **B:** Exceeding weight affects performance but does not directly cause a stall — angle of attack does.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1b | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 5; ACS UA.IV.A.K1b | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: stall, angle-of-attack, aerodynamics_

---

#### LP-015

**Q:** What is the definition of angle of attack?

- **A.** The angle between the aircraft's longitudinal axis and the horizon
- **B.** The angle between the aircraft's flight path and the horizon
- **C.** The angle between the chord line of the wing or propeller blade and the relative wind ✅
- **D.** The angle of bank during a coordinated turn

**✅ Answer:** C — The angle between the chord line of the wing or propeller blade and the relative wind

**Explanation:** Angle of attack (AOA) is defined as the angle between the chord line of a wing or propeller blade and the direction of the relative wind (oncoming airflow). As AOA increases, lift increases — up to the critical angle of attack, beyond which the wing stalls.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** That describes the pitch attitude of the aircraft, not angle of attack.
- **B:** That describes the flight path angle relative to the horizon, not angle of attack.
- **D:** Bank angle is an entirely different aerodynamic concept related to turning flight.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 5 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: angle-of-attack, chord-line, relative-wind, aerodynamics_

---

### Altimetry

#### PRAC-013

**Q:** When an altimeter setting is adjusted to 29.92 inches of mercury, what altitude is being displayed?

- **A.** True altitude.
- **B.** Density altitude.
- **C.** Pressure altitude. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — Pressure altitude.

**Explanation:** Setting 29.92 inches of mercury in the altimeter window causes the instrument to indicate pressure altitude, which is the altitude above the standard datum plane.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** True altitude is the aircraft's actual height above mean sea level after correcting for nonstandard conditions.
- **B:** Density altitude is pressure altitude corrected for nonstandard temperature.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 11 | Source: User Question Intake 2026-03-14 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: unmanned aircraft, altimeter, pressure altitude, performance_

---

### Center of Gravity

#### LP-013

**Q:** How does a drone with its center of gravity too far forward typically behave in flight?

- **A.** It tends to pitch up and may become difficult to control at high speeds
- **B.** It tends to pitch down, requiring front motors to work harder to maintain level flight and climb ✅
- **C.** It drifts to one side due to asymmetric lift
- **D.** It becomes more stable and easier to fly

**✅ Answer:** B — It tends to pitch down, requiring front motors to work harder to maintain level flight and climb

**Explanation:** When a drone's center of gravity (CG) is too far forward, the nose-heavy condition causes the aircraft to pitch down. The front motors must constantly work harder to counteract this tendency to maintain level flight and climb, reducing efficiency and flight time.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** A forward CG causes a pitch-down tendency, not pitch-up.
- **C:** Lateral drift is caused by asymmetric loading, not a forward CG.
- **D:** An improper CG location reduces stability and performance — it does not improve handling.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K2 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 10 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: center-of-gravity, forward-cg, pitch-down, loading_

---

### Extended Terms

#### EXT-017

**Q:** What is density altitude and how does it affect performance?

- **A.** Pressure altitude corrected for nonstandard temperature. High density altitude reduces lift, climb performance, and battery margin. ✅
- **B.** The altitude at which an aircraft must fly for maximum fuel efficiency.
- **C.** The altitude measured from sea level based on air density alone.

**✅ Answer:** A — Pressure altitude corrected for nonstandard temperature. High density altitude reduces lift, climb performance, and battery margin.

**Explanation:** Density altitude is pressure altitude corrected for nonstandard temperature. High density altitude (hot, high, humid conditions) reduces lift, climb performance, and often battery margin for electric drones.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Density altitude is a performance indicator, not an efficiency altitude.
- **C:** Density altitude combines pressure and temperature effects — it is not simply an MSL reading.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 11 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: performance, density-altitude, weather_

---

#### EXT-023

**Q:** What is the Center of Gravity (CG) in aviation?

- **A.** The balance point of the aircraft system. An improper CG can degrade stability and performance. ✅
- **B.** The maximum takeoff weight limit.
- **C.** The geometric center of the aircraft's fuselage.

**✅ Answer:** A — The balance point of the aircraft system. An improper CG can degrade stability and performance.

**Explanation:** CG (Center of Gravity) is the balance point of the aircraft system. If the CG is outside allowable limits it can degrade stability, handling, and performance.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Maximum takeoff weight is a separate specification from CG.
- **C:** CG is the balance point based on weight distribution, not the geometric center.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 10 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: performance, cg, loading_

---

#### EXT-024

**Q:** What is Load Factor?

- **A.** The ratio of aerodynamic load supported by the aircraft to the aircraft's weight. Higher load factor increases aerodynamic demand during maneuvering. ✅
- **B.** The maximum payload the aircraft can carry.
- **C.** The ratio of battery capacity to flight time.

**✅ Answer:** A — The ratio of aerodynamic load supported by the aircraft to the aircraft's weight. Higher load factor increases aerodynamic demand during maneuvering.

**Explanation:** Load factor is the ratio of aerodynamic load supported by the wings or rotors to the aircraft's weight. Higher load factors occur during turns and maneuvering and increase aerodynamic demand.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** Payload capacity is a separate specification. Load factor is a ratio, not a weight.
- **C:** Load factor is an aerodynamic concept, not a battery metric.

_ACS: UA.IV.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25B Ch. 5 | Source: Part 107 Extended Terms | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: performance, load-factor, aerodynamics_

---

### Load Factors

#### LP-001

**Q:** (Refer to FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 2.) If an unmanned airplane weighs 33 pounds, what approximate weight would the airplane structure be required to support during a 30° banked turn while maintaining altitude?

📊 _Refer to: figure-2_

- **A.** 34 pounds.
- **B.** 47 pounds.
- **C.** 38 pounds. ✅

**✅ Answer:** C — 38 pounds.

**Explanation:** In a 30° banked turn, the load factor is approximately 1.15. So 33 lbs × 1.15 ≈ 38 pounds. The load factor chart (Figure 2) shows this relationship between bank angle and the G-forces on the structure.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** 34 pounds would only represent a load factor of about 1.03, which corresponds to a bank much less than 30°.
- **B:** 47 pounds would represent a load factor of about 1.43, which corresponds to approximately a 45° bank, not 30°.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1a | Citation: FAA-CT-8080-2H, Figure 2; ACS UA.IV.A.K1a | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: load-factor, banked-turn, weight_

---

#### LP-002

**Q:** When operating an unmanned airplane, the remote pilot should consider that the load factor on the wings may be increased any time

- **A.** the CG is shifted rearward to the aft CG limit.
- **B.** the airplane is subjected to maneuvers other than straight-and-level flight. ✅
- **C.** the gross weight is reduced.

**✅ Answer:** B — the airplane is subjected to maneuvers other than straight-and-level flight.

**Explanation:** Any time an aircraft deviates from straight-and-level flight — during turns, climbs, turbulence encounters, or pull-ups — the load factor increases above 1G, placing additional stress on the structure.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** CG position affects stability and control, but does not by itself increase load factor.
- **C:** Reducing gross weight actually reduces the structural loads, not increases them.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1a | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 5; ACS UA.IV.A.K1a | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: load-factor, maneuvering, structural-limits_

---

### Performance

#### LP-014

**Q:** How does increasing drone weight affect stall speed and overall flight performance?

- **A.** Higher weight decreases stall speed and improves stability
- **B.** Higher weight has no effect on stall speed
- **C.** Higher weight increases stall speed and negatively affects overall performance ✅
- **D.** Higher weight only affects battery life, not aerodynamic performance

**✅ Answer:** C — Higher weight increases stall speed and negatively affects overall performance

**Explanation:** A heavier aircraft requires more lift to maintain flight, which means it must fly at a higher angle of attack — leading to a higher stall speed. Greater weight also increases power required, reduces endurance and range, decreases climb performance, and generally degrades all aspects of flight performance.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **A:** Higher weight increases stall speed — the opposite of what this choice states.
- **B:** Weight directly determines the lift required and therefore directly affects stall speed.
- **D:** While battery life is affected by weight, the aerodynamic performance impacts are equally significant.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 10 | Source: Review.md | Difficulty: 2/5 | Tags: weight, stall-speed, performance, loading_

---

### Weight & Balance

#### LP-004

**Q:** To ensure that the unmanned aircraft center of gravity (CG) limits are not exceeded, follow the aircraft loading instructions specified in the

- **A.** Pilot's Operating Handbook or UAS Flight Manual. ✅
- **B.** Aeronautical Information Manual (AIM).
- **C.** Aircraft Weight and Balance Handbook.

**✅ Answer:** A — Pilot's Operating Handbook or UAS Flight Manual.

**Explanation:** The Pilot's Operating Handbook (POH) or UAS Flight Manual contains the specific CG limits, loading instructions, and weight and balance data for the particular aircraft being operated.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** The AIM contains general flight information and ATC procedures but not aircraft-specific loading data.
- **C:** The Aircraft Weight and Balance Handbook is a general reference; the specific aircraft's POH or Flight Manual is the authoritative source.

_ACS: UA.IV.A.K1b | Citation: FAA-H-8083-25, Ch 10; ACS UA.IV.A.K1b | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: center-of-gravity, weight-balance, flight-manual_

## Crew Resource Management

**9 questions**

### Acronyms & Abbreviations

#### ACR-006

**Q:** What does "ADM" stand for?

- **A.** Aeronautical Decision-Making. ✅
- **B.** Aircraft Departure Management.
- **C.** Automated Data Monitoring.

**✅ Answer:** A — Aeronautical Decision-Making.

**Explanation:** ADM stands for Aeronautical Decision-Making — a systematic process for identifying hazards, assessing risk, and choosing safe courses of action.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** ADM does not refer to departure management. It is a decision-making framework.
- **C:** ADM is not about data monitoring. It is a structured approach to aviation risk management.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-2 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, adm, risk-management_

#### ACR-019

**Q:** What does "CRM" stand for?

- **A.** Crew Resource Management. ✅
- **B.** Crash Recovery Mission.
- **C.** Controlled Route Mapping.

**✅ Answer:** A — Crew Resource Management.

**Explanation:** CRM stands for Crew Resource Management — the effective use of all available resources (people, equipment, information) to improve safety and decision making during flight operations.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** CRM is not about crash recovery. It focuses on proactive safety through resource management.
- **C:** CRM has nothing to do with route mapping. It is a team-based safety management approach.

_ACS: UA.V.B.K1 | Citation: FAA-H-8083-2 | Source: FAA Remote Pilot Study Guide Appendix 3 | Difficulty: 1/5 | Tags: acronym, crm, safety_

#### ACR-054

**Q:** What does "PAVE" stand for as a risk assessment checklist?

- **A.** PIC (or Pilot) – Aircraft – enVironment – External pressures. ✅
- **B.** Procedure – Altitude – Visibility – Equipment.
- **C.** Plan – Assess – Verify – Execute.

**✅ Answer:** A — PIC (or Pilot) – Aircraft – enVironment – External pressures.

**Explanation:** PAVE stands for Pilot, Aircraft, enVironment, External pressures — a structured checklist for assessing risk before and during flight.

**Why other answers are wrong:**
- **B:** PAVE does not include altitude or visibility. It evaluates four risk categories.
- **C:** PAVE is not a process flow. It categorizes risk into Pilot, Aircraft, enVironment, and External pressures.
